THE FREE CHURCHES.

The agitation against the Education kill! shows no diminution in enthusiasm and deter- mination. The most crucial clauses of the Bill are those which deal with the management of voluntary schools. Free Churchmen insist that the representatives of the ratepayers should form the majority of the managers, while the Govesnment and the Church party are not willing to concede more than one-third. In- deed, the House of Laymen in the Province of Canterbury has passed a resolution deblaring that to go farther would make the Bill injurious to the interests of the Church. At the present moment it seems certain that if the Government persist they will find themselves faced early next year with a serious No-rate Campaign extending from the Tweed to Land's End. Many of the most eminent Free Churchmen, ministerial and lay, have publicly declared that they will never consent to pay rates for cleri- cally-managed schools. The National Free Church Council has followed the progress of the BM with the greatest vigilance, and the situation is now felt to be so serious that a special meet- ing of the General Committee is to be held im- _ mediately. In the ordinary course the Com- mittee would not meet until October.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR ALFRED THOMAS, M.P.

The best known Nonconformists among the recipients of Coronation honours are Sir Alfred Thomas, M.P., the Chairman of the Welsh Par- liamentary party, and Sir Horace B. Marshall, the well-known publisher. Sir Alfred Thomas regards his knighthood as a compliment to Wales, but it is also a marked and well-deserved compliment to himself. Sir Alfred, who is a native of Cardiff, and 62 years of age, is a Baptist, as his father was before him, and has been Chairman of the Welsh Baptist Union! He has done great things for many Welsh move- ments, particularly for the University of Wales. It is owing to him more than to any other man that the University College of South Wales is situated in Cardiff. Personally, Sir Alfred is genial and unassuming, very generous and very popular with all who know him. He is a bachelor. An interesting sketch of Sir Alfred, with portrait, appears in the Free Church Chronicle for August.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR HORACE MARSHALL.

Sir Horace Marshall, who is this year one of the Sheriffs of London, is a devoted Wesleyan- Meth&list, and the publisher of the Methodist Times. We believe that every year he returns his profits from that paper to the funds of the connexion. Sir Horace, though a very busy man, and the head of a great commercial house, has many literary and scholarly interests, and has recently received the degree of LL.D. from Trinity College, Dublin. It is well known that this Irish University is very discriminating in its distribution of honours.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. PARKER.

We are very pleased to hear that Dr. Parker is making good progress towards recovery. It will not surprise those who know Dr. Parker to learn that the chief difficulty of his medical adviser ia to persuade him to take complete rent. Dr. Parker is now allowed on fine days to leave his room, and to recline on a couch in his beautiful garden. Dr. Parker has always been very fond of the open air, and raany a great sermon has he meditated while in this garden-room, with, the vault of heaven for a roof. Dr. Parker's friends are hopeful that his voice will again be heard in the autumn from his beloved pulpit in the City Temple.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD SALISBURY AND NONCONFORMISTS.

The Prime Minister who has just retired though he Compelled the respect and admiration of 'Free Churchmen, never showed much know- ledge of Nonconformity, nor much sympathy with it. On one occasion he referred to Non- conformists as "people of a different religion" from Church people. The Rev. Hugh Price Hughes, M.A., was on one occasion a member of a deputation that waited upon Lord Salis- bury, and the Prime Minister referred to him as Mr. Hugh Hughes. It is not to be supposed for a moment that this was studied discourtesy; it was merely an exhibition of Lord Salisbury's defective acquaintance with the Free Churches. By-the-way, Mr. Hughes is much better and hopes to resume full work in the autumn.

THE REV. A. CONNELL'S MARRIAGE.

Much interest has been taken in all Presby- terian circles in the marriage of the Rev. Alexander Connell, 8.D., of Regent's-square Church, London, to Miss Jessie M. Robson, the only daughter of Mr. Henry Robson. Mr. Connell succeeded the well-known Evangelist, John McNeill, in the ministry of Regent's- square, and is exceedingly popular with his congregation. He is noted not only for his preaching ability, but for his attractive pre- sence and charming manners. Indeed, he has been described as the possessor of the finest manners in London. It is an open secret that a well-known lady novelist, a Scotswoman, has made him the hero of one of her stories. Mr. Robson, the father of the bride, is a stock- broker, a member of the firm of Renton Bros., and one of the richest men in the English Presbyterian Church. He once unsuccessfully contested Forfarshire in the Liberal interest. Mr. and Mrs. Connell are spending their honeymoon in Norway, Scotland, and Ireland.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
THE QUEEN AND THE GIP.LS' GUILD,

About a hundred servants, members of ;the London Free Church Girls' Guilds, were enter- tained by her Majesty the Queen to tea. The entertainment took place at the beautiful house of Alderman Evan Spicer, J.P., L.C.C., in Dulwich. The Queen was unable to be present, but sent the Duchess of Portland to act in her stead. Her Grace won the hearts of all the girls by her charm and kindness. Mrs. Charlotte Benham, the organiser of the Girls' Guild, superintended the arrangements. The National Council paid the travelling expenses of the girls to and from Mr. Spicer's house. There are now 130 Guilds with 10,000 members.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. E. G. CANOE.

Rev. E. G. Gange, who is resigning the pasto- rate of Regent's-park Baptist Church, is an erudite and expert astronomer, and had an observatory erected on the roof of his house. There were, perhaps, more doctors in Mr. Gange's congregation than in any Noncon- formist church in the country. Quite a little group of them used to assemble in the vestry of a Sunday morning. -• aro"' -

ILLUSTRATED FASHIONS.

Among the prettiest of the pretty materials offered for our selection this summer are the dainty silks for blouses or dresses which are further embellished by lace or transparent stripes. White with a black lace strite or rice versa are among the most popular and make up into very effective toilettes. Neither are they expensive. The same idea is also carried out in different kinds of cotton fabrics at, of course, a lesser degree of cost. For a matron or middle-aged woman a black sateen with stripes of twine- coloured lace made over a white foundation looks very smart indeed. Thin woollen materials are made in something of the same style, only the transparent stripe is generally made of silk threads woven into ten open, well defined pattern. Taffeta looks particularly well, and is very much in vogue both plain and with the popular lace stripe. A very charming design which is new this season, shows a narrow coloured silk stripe between wider graduated stripes of white open work. Louisine moire is also a fashionable novelty, as is velvet grenadine printed in all colours. British Bilks have had much demand In consequence of the wish expressed by the Queen that the ladies of her Court should patronise home-made materials when selecting dresses for the Coronation and the many other social events which this and every other season brings. Of course, most of the silks

===== 0002601_19020725_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
Mirl7l. DUE:

are named after Her Majesty, a token, it is to be supposed, of the gratitude of the home manufac- turers. The useful dress sketched is of grey sateen spotted with black dots, trimmed with ecru face, a touch of colour being introduced by a chou 'of bright, yet pale green velvet. The hat which completes the costume is trimmed with green ribbon intermingled with that travesty in nature, a black rose, also an osprey, the straw itself being of a burnt tint. The dress should, to prove quite a success, be lined with pale green silk or sateen, with a' frill round the inside of the hem.

Lace seems only to 'increase in popularity as a finish to every article of the toilette with thE advance of time, and never, I suppose, were there so many varieties of lace in Vogue as this season In some of the country districts the lace industry has been started with great success, providing many women with a pleasant means of earning au honest livelihood all the year in their own homes. Devon has, of course, been long noted fen Honiton point and Honiton applique, but no% this industry has been started and has met with much success in Norfolk. The pretty coal sketched is of lace trimmed with a little chiffor to soften i. These lace coats are worn this season in various lengths, from the short bolerc to the long three-quarter coat. They imake e pretty and at the same time a cool wrap fos smmmer days, and often give a finish to a gown

:ETTY COAT.

which it would otherwise lack. Irish guipure is one of the most fashionable and popular laces. but imitations of antique needlepoint laces and combinations of two or three different laces produce a beautiful whole and are much in vogue. The indented Alencon lace is much in favour, and is made in very beautiful and striking patterns. Medallion designs are a leading feature in the novelties, both square and round medallions being often combined. A very charming and chic trimming for a summer gown just introduced this season consists of Irish guipure motifs between oblong pieces of embroidered white glace. Two shades of lace are in great variety—ecru and white—Paris and ivory, and so on. The most gorgeous trimming of a mingled variety that I have seen consisted of Irish guipure edged with ecru. arranged as a sort of frame to medallions of white satin embroidered in Watteau figures in coloured silks. The effect was lovely, but the price per yard placed it quite out of reach of most people. Tinsel and silk embroidery in Oriental colourings are frequently introduced in a white or ecru guipure galon.

The charming hat illustrated is trimmed with cherries, but a black bird is substituted in place of the usual bow of black velvet ribbon, of which we are now somewhat wearied. The hat itself is of a golden Tuscan straw, and the com- bination of colours is one most pleasing to the eye. The wearing of birds of large dimension

4ie a feature of the millinery of the moment, and Paradise sweeping plumes are often to be seen in spite of the outcry made against them by some people. Among the new straws the Chrysanthe- mum," the " Astracan " and the "Frisette " are all pretty, and lend themselves to the styles now in rogue. As a trimming, this is essentially a fruit on, but among the flowers most popular are :he pompon roses, which appear everywhere, and ire for the most part arranged in little wreaths. These wreaths are considered very modish and some- times two are worn, one running into the other and interlacing. COQUETTE.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

At one time women were eligible to be Knights of thli 1 Garter, and as Edward 111, founded the Order in 1349, so it VMS hoeed that King Edward VII. might restore its ancient privileges to the fair sex, outside of the Court. The modern woman with her abilities and inde- pendence, has advanced so rapidly in many distinctions, that it is sometimes forgotten tbet she has lost any, but at least down to the reign of EdirardVl.tbere were Lady Knights if the Gaiter. These included among-t others, the Countestes of Buckieghane, Pembroke, Salisbury, Derby, Arundel, Warwick, Pembroke, and Richmond Charles I. proposed to restore the privileges to women, and similar proposals have been made subsequently, but they bays all come te nothing. Our late Quote). as Sovereign of the Order,ueed frequently to wear the stet and riband on State occasions, and Queen Alexandra is now the only Lady Knight of the Garter. The King's illness has not only made his Majests much thinner, but the serious nature of the operation, and the shock to the system, will in all probability Ie&VA a permanent impression upon the countenance. It is all but inevitable that these traces will remain even though as seems very likely, the King may enjoy better health than be has dome for yearieas soon as his strength is restored. Noels it surprising that ou: ever youthful Queen Should show some evidence of the great strain and anxiety she has undergone. It must also he remembered that her MajB9to public life has been more strenuous that it was when she was Princess of Wales. There is the greater pomp, and splendour, and ceremonial, but if her Majesty could have her way, she would infinitely prefer the quiet life she used to lead at Sandringham. Mr Balfour is one of those confirmed bachelors whom the gossips long since gave up in despair. They used, in years gone by, to link his name with that of an un- married lady, but as nothing ever came of it, they gave up this unprofitable speculation. It was the same with the late Mr Cecil Rhodes, though he was supposed to be indifferent to women's society, and no one took seriously the suggestions that be thought of marrying. The same suspicion hangs over Lord Kitchener, who, from a social standpoint, is, and is likely to he, a much sought-after guest, in the highest ranks of society. Hitherto ths gossips have mot taken any grave liberties with his name, but present temptations are not unlikely to prove Itoo much for them, As also in the came of Mr halftime Both these able and distinguished men come from marry- ing families, and this is something to go upon, as the Premier is the only bachelor in his family, and Lord Kitchener's father and grandfather were both twice married. ileThe spell of beautiful summer weather has been wel- come to the drapers, as there is nothing like sunshine for encouraging shopping and tradespeople have only the ready-money customer to look to, at this season of the year. The clearance sales have been crowded with costomere, and there has been a huge demand for muslin and linen gowns, and light summer milinery. It is astonishing to note at these sales, how many well-to-de women dress themselves on the ready-made principle. Instead:of buying lengths of material and handing them over to the—frequently—ineonspetent dressmaker, it is bscotning more and more the custom to buy the costume complete and the demand for the well-cut ready-mado skirt and bolero, is now almost as great as for Has universal blouse. At the larger establishments there it usually a wide choice of many styles and designs, and even if one is not quite "stock size," the purchases ea* be adapted to any figure, if not quite as perfectly as if "made ti erder." Some idea of the exharbitant charges of fathienable dressmakers may he gathered from a recent law snit in Pane. A woman of rank who had spent about £12,000 or £13,000 at her dressmakers, during the past two or three years, asked for a reduction of £5OO on the score of exeersive charges. This request was refused,end the case was takeu into Court, where it was shown that as much as £4 Sr £5 had been charged for simply sewing on a button,and two etr three times this amount far trifling alterations which a working dressmaker could do in an hour. Tradespeople are net to be blamed for asking as much as they can get, but one can only mare,' that women of wealth and fashion can find no better use fcr their money, than wasting it in this manner. Vanity almort surpasses itself in the latest style of bathing cap. WILMAY,

BY BARRY PAIN.

PART IV. -CRAPTER X.

"I am afraid," said Charles Forland, nervously, "that you must find my presence a little em- barrassing." "Not at all," I lied, civilly. "I should like to assure you that I have no feelings of resentment against you whatever. It is not your fault that my poor brother-in-law gave you a position—the position of Wilmay's guardian —which should have been mine. With all hie kindness to me he misunderstood me, and I gather from his solicitor's, whom I have just left, that at the last he did not even mention my name in his will."

"I happen to know that he believed you to be dead." _

"Yes, I daresay it was my fault." He spoke with more confidence now. His manner was by no means bad. He was not quite the suave hypocrite that I had expected; indeed, he showed one or two touches of cynicism that rather amused me. "However, I, his brother-in-law, re ;eived nothing; you, a stranger, benefited considerably.° "Did the solicitors tell you that ? " "Do solicitors ever tell you anything that you want to know? A man, -however, does not make you tha guardian of his child, and--" "There is no mystery about it. If you want to know, Philip left me his library and his cellar. I'm sorry it should annoy you." "But, Mr. Derrimer, it does not annoy me. I have been trying to explain that, though under the circumstances I might feel resentment, I feel none. In fact it is to you, as Wilmay's guardian, that I come first. I might have gone to Wilthay or fir Vincent—of course I have read in the papers of the impending marriage—but I -went to Philip's solicitors and, learning from them that you were WiLay.'s guardian, decided to apply to you first."

"What have you been doing all the time that you were away ? ' "I cannot tell you all the details, Shortly after I last saw Philip—it was an occasion on which he had very much exasperated me—l left England for America. I had resolved not to permit Vhilip to advance any more money for the purposes of speculative business of my own, which hitherto had been unremunerative, but still contained great promises. I resolved to adopt a quite different course. I said to myself that I would now make a fortune for myself and for no one else, and leave Philip out of it. As I say, at the time I was exasperated against Philip." As I have said, the man was not exactly a hypo- rite; be was a swindler who began by deceiving himse t.

"Did you ever eat a Compactum Dinner?" he asked, "No. I don't understand."

"It was a novelty in canned goods. A tin with four compartments--soup in the first, fish in the second, roast meat in tin§ third, and vegetables in the last. A Compactum Dinner, sufficient for one person, could at one time have been purchased in London for a shilling." "No doubt," I said, " it was very good, but what's it got to do with it?" "Good? It was bad. I may say that it was damned bad. And I ought to know, for I invented it, manufactured it, exported it, made a fortune over it, went bankrupt over it, and was finally brought so low that I once tried to eat it. And in that sentence I've given you the history of three years. My next bankruptcy was three years later, and very unexpected. My third—" "Never mind," I said, drearily, "going into all the bankruptcies." "I merely wanted to show you that I did work and struggle, whatever Philip said of my idleness. Philip also said that I should die of drink, and in a moment of irritation expressed the hope that I would do it soon. I must show you that his version of my character was not the right one. I have my self-respect." "All right," I said, "I see that you don't drink now, Mr. Forland. But had we better not come to business? Why have you applied to me?" "Because I thought it possible that you might not wish me to apply to Wilmay or Sir. 'Vincent." "Shall I speak quite plainly?" • "Certainly." "I should prefer that you never saw either, and never communicated with either in any way." "Yes," he said, "you have spoken plainly, and I will speak as plainly. I have no recollection of Wilmay, and I have never seen Sir Vincent Carrone. As far as my affection for them is con- cerned, it does not matter one straw to me if I never see either, and they are left in ignorance of my existence. It is on a point of principle that I should see them. I feel myself a debtor to them; it is my duty, and I feel it to be my duty, to repay them every penny of the money that my sister and Philip advanced to me. I have kept a note of the exact sum, six thousand five hundred pounds, and I wish to repay it." For the moment I was very much surprised, but it was only for the moment. " I need not say that I have not the money," he went on. "But they can very well afford to advance me the thousand that will enable me to get that and much more. lam her mother's bro- ther, and her father was exceedingly kind to me until an unfortunate misunderstanding. I have a claim."

"Look here," I said, "you have told me you once made a fortune. Why didn't you repay then?" "I was on the point of doing so. But I was extending my business at the time. Extend a business too far, and it breaks—er—like—elastic. Mine broke." I walked up and down the room. For Wi lm ay's sake I had to get this man out of England. I would speak to Sir Vincent about him, because I knew he would take a common-sense view of the case, and because it seemed unfair not to tell him. But I wanted to keep Forlancl away from Wilmay, and not to let the relationship become publicly known. "Where would you go if you had money ?" I asked. " Coolgardie," he replied, without the least hesi- tation. " Well," I said, "I'm glad you came to me. Wilmay has no power at present to give you the money. Sir Vincent wouldn't give you it. I can and I will. lam going to write a cheque for two hundred, and I will pay the balance of eight hundred pounds to you in instalments of one hundred every three months. Should you, during that period, at any time return to England, or communicate in any way with Wilmay or Sir Vincent Carrone, the remainder due to you will be stopped." He said that he was reluctant to take money from me, as he felt that on me personally he had no claim whatever. He hoped that I would regard it as an investment—simply as an investment. In short, he accepted the arrangement, and pocketed the cheque. "And now," he said, "you must dine with me." For the fun of the thing, I did dine with him, and I have never been better entertained. He told stories about himself, and I have no doubt that he lied, but he lied most amusingly. He kept a glas-7. of wine by his side at dinner, but he never touched it, and drank only water. A few days afterwards I had the pleasure of seeing him off. "Pardon me," he said at the last moment, "but I know you better now, and I want to ask you something. You have heard Philip speak of me, apparently—you have now had an opportunity of judging for yourself. Do you think lam a dammed blackguard ? ' I replied that we were all the creatures of circum- stances. He sighed, and seemed dissatisfied. To Sir Vincent I merely said that Wilmay, though she did not know it, had an uncle Who was a bad lot. "So've I," he answered. "Everybody's got a bad egg of a relation somewhere." " Well," I said, " this particular egg is on its way to Coolgardie at present. Its favourite pas- times are borrowing money, and occasionally entering on some weird business and going bank- rupt. If it should ever apply to you, I want you to do nothing until you have consulted me." "Very well, as you like. But as I say, couldn't one do anything for the poor beggar?" "Very good of you, but it's really not necessary. At present he's provided for. I mentioned it, because it was the particular wish of her father that Wilmay should never see this man or know of his existence. She is sensitive—it would humiliate and distress her. I thought a word to you—" "Of course. Much obliged to you. The world's too bad for Wilmay anyhow, and the less she knows of the badness the better."

CHAPTER XI.

It was about a week before this conversation took place that I saw Wilmay for.the first time (with the exception of that momentary glimpse in the park) since my return to England. It was at Bertha's house in the morning. I had sent word that I was coming, and I found them both in the morning-room. Bertha looked very young and quite pretty, and greeted me with much sisterly effusiveness. Wilmay took my hand, and looked away from me, and said, "Edward, I am so glad —so glad that you have come back." As we talked I watched Wilmay. The glimpse that I had had of her in the park had been decep- tive. Driving in the fresh wind must have brought the colour to her cheeks, and I suppose even if one is sad at heart one still laughs passably well in public if one's companion says an amusing thing: Now she was very pale, and when she was not talking or conscious that anyone was looking at her, her expression was Wistful and her eyes seemed fixed on something far away. "Wilmay," I said, "are you really quite well?" "Oh, quite!" "There is nothing the matter with her," said Bertha, "but she is tir6d. She has to go about so much, and see so many people. There are Carrones innumerable, all anxious to inspect her." "If she's quite well," I said, "she doesn't look it. And unless she can manage to look it, she shan't be married at all." "Please do not let us talk about me any more," said Wilmay. "I want to know all about the opera. We've heard it, of course. Everybody's heard of it, and everybody's talking of it. But I want to hear the history of it." "And I'm sick to death of the opera," I replied, "and I want to hear the history of your engage- ..

ment." But on that point Wilmay was absolutely mute, and let Bertha answer for her. As the days went on, and I s w more of Wilmay, I grew more uneasy. She admired Sir Vincent and was fond of him in a way, but it did not strike me that she was in love with him. Sometimes I thought I saw a doubt on his honest, handsome face. Sometimes even Bertha herself would look a little anxious when she spoke of Wilmay.

The time sped—flew, iti seemed to nie—mors Flinty to catastrophe, as a moth to a name. it was Less than a week before the day fixed for the mar- riage that I got a note from Wilmay in the after- noon . " DEAR EDWARD,—We were to have dined with Lady Harstonto-night, but Lady Harston has been the angel to have one of her neuralgic attacks (poor dear!) and put everyone off. We have a night's peace. Bertha and you and I dine together here, and you play to us afterwards. You will, won't you? Not even Vincent will be here. We are quite alone, and we both want to see you. Do be good to your loving Bertha and \Varney." I went, of course. When I got into the drawing. room I noticed that the piano was no longer used like the back yard of a curiosity-shop, but was allowed to be merely a piano. "No," said Bertha, "you needn't give me the credit or the discredit of it. That's Wilmay. I suppose when you've written great operas you expect that kind of homage?" I have not written great operas, but I have a tiresome and offensive sister: which sounds like 011endorf, but is only the gospel truth. Wilmay, lam indebted to you. I will talk to you, to the entire exclusion of that elderly female at whose house you board." "Oh, do stop ! " cried Bertha. "You shouldn't say. things like that to anyone with any-imagina- tion. I feel as if I lived in Bloomsbury and charged extra for the boots. I feel old, and dishonest, and haggard." Do you," I said. "Well, we realise the truth about ourselves sooner or later."

"Very well," Bertha retorted, and order Mr. Derrimer's carriage." "Mr. Derrimer came in a cab, I replied, "and he will not go until he has had • dinner, anyhow. And, by the w9,y—" Carter opened the door, I gave my arm to Wilmay, and we passed into the dining-room. We were all three in a state of pleasant but foolish high spirits. Wilmay was particularly audacious; her eyes were very bright, and her colour a little feverish. She told us a beautiful story of how she had really composed that opera, but I had stolen it, and she had never exposed me in order to save me the pain of knowing that I had for my ward a girl whose guardian was a thief. But all through dinner she never made the least allusion to her approach- ing marriage, and when Bertha spoke of it Wilmay changed the subject. After dinner I went to the piano. The evening of Wilmay's sixteenth birthday came back to my mind. She was wearing the string of little pearls that I had given her then. At first, I looked at her from time to time as I played. She sat half in shadow, her lips a little parted, her eyes fixed. There had always been something of another world about her, and to-night as she sat listening to the music she seemed more than ever ethereal, spiritual. Her marriage turned to an insult, a tragedy, in my mind. I took my eyes from her, and became lost in the music. I was playing the Chopin nocturne that I had played on the night of her birthday. Suddenly I heard a rustling of dresses. The door opened, and closed again quickly. I stopped playing and looked up. Bertha and Wilmay had gone, and I was alone in the room. An indefinable sense of horror and pain came over me. I was glad when a minute or so later Bertha came back again. She looked troubled. "What is the matter ? " I asked. "What hak happened '3" Did you not see?" No.)

"I shall ring

" Wilmay began to cry, and got up. I went out with her, and I've left her upstairs. She told to come down again." "Is she still crying ? " "Yes, she is lying on her bed and sobbing violently. She does not seem able to tell me what" the matter with her. Indeed, she say's that noth ing's the matter." • " There must be some explanation." "She is in a nervous, over-wrought state, and music always has a great effect upon her. But I do not understand it myself. I have never seen her lose her self-control like this before. And thought that at dinner she seemed unusually bright and happy." "Is anyone with her now?" "Yes, I think Mrs. Blayd is there." Bertha sighed. "Oh, this is horrible 1" she exclaimed, petulantly. • There was a long pause. Bertha sat with her head on her hand. I paced the room, and then stopped. Before I could speak Bertha burst out—. "I know what you're going to say, Edward, I know what you're going to say." "Very likely," I replied, ." but I shall say it. Wilmay's marriage must not take place. She is not in love with Vincent. You must break it off. There is time yet, and you or I must do it." "I don't know whether it will surprise you, but

since your return, only a few days'ago, I asked Wilmay if she would like me to break it off. She said most emphatically that she would not hear .of it, that she had considered everything when she accepted. She even asked me not to speak of it again. But to-night, just now when I took her upstairs, I did hint at it. She would only say, No ! no!' and once, Why must I make people unhappy ? ' It's not my fault, is it? lam so fond of Wilmay." Bertha looked at me with tears in her eyes. "No," I said, " you need not blame yourself. I am glad you suggested it. Now we can do noth- ing—she is no longer a child, and she must make her own choice. But somehow I can't bring myself to think that this marriage is really going to take place." It 'levee did take place. On the morning before the day, I received a note from Bertha asking me to come round at once. I found Bertha looking white and ill, with a letter in her hands. "Wilmay has gone," she said at once. "Gone?" " Yes, left London. Thank heaven, she has Mrs. Blayd with her. She went early this morn- ing before I was up, and this note was brought to me."

I took the note and glanced through it. "In the night, Bertha, I found that I could not do it. I had to go away. I got up and found Mrs. Blayd, and talked to her, and shall leave London early this morning. I have written to Vincent. And I will write to you again, when I know where we shall be. Will you please let me be quite alone for some time? Oh, dear Bertha, Ido love you so much. Do, do forgive me! As soon as you get the address, write and tell roe that ' you are not angry. I cannot help it. I should go mad if I didn't go away." I had a long talk with Bertha. It was publicly announced that owing to the illness of Miss Amory her marriage with Sir Vincent Carrone was postponed. Later and more gradually it became known that the marriage would not take place at all. Vincent behaved finely. He would not allow

anyone to say a worcr against wirmay: war quarrelled lastingly with his own brother on tne subject. To me he said, "Edward "—by this time he had got into the habit of calling me by my first name—" it's bad, but it's best for her. She trisut to love me, I know that. If I can ever do anything, in some otker way, to make her happy, you will let me know. That is really all I want now—it is horrible to think of her as being unhappy, and that lam the cause of it."

He had the luck some five years afterwards to be killed in the hunting field.

CHAPTER XII.

When Wilmay and Mrs. Blayd left London, they went to Starley, a south-coast village where, I believe, Mrs. Blayd had some relations. Before they had been there very long, what had been merely an excuse became the actual fact. Wilmay was seriously ill. Bertha and I went down to Starley then, and took a specialist with us. He said that in some respects the case puzzled him, but he seemed quite hopeful, and suggested Men. tone for the winter. I saw Wilmay only for a few minutes on this occasion, and she spoke very little. It was terrible to see the change that had taken place in her. Bertha and Wilmay went away together. I remained in London, working hard at my second opera. I heard frequently from Bertha, and the reports of Wiltnay's health grew worse and worse. She was longing to get back to England, and her greatest desire was, to live at Sinden again. After a good deal of consultation it was decided to let her have her own way, supposing that I could make the necessary arrangements. At this time Sinden was let on lease, and when the tfina.nt wired to give up the remainder of his lease he :iret laughed at me, and then got angry and said that he was insulted. However, Wilmay was wealthy !,nough to allow me to use a form of argument which does not often fail, and it did not fail in this sase. So in the spring Bertha and Wilmay came back to the house where Phillip Amory died. Bertha refused to leave Wilmay now—even for the 4elights of the London season. About this time I had a letter from Charles Por- tend. I had heard from him once before' but that was merely to give the name of the bank to which Lie wished me to pay the money to his credit. This mond letter was very different, and a great sur- prise to me. It returned me all the money that I sclvanced to him with five per cent, interest. It slso enclosed one thousand pounds, whicill was to be paid to Wilmay as a tirst instalment in discharge of the debt which he had incurred to her father. _ _ _

"It may be," he wrote, "that when I am poor I am 'a blackguard, but I am quite certain that when lam rich Ipm a sentimentalist. For, of course, I am under nb legal obligation to pay Wilmay any. thing, I choose to consider myself in debt to her, but the money advanced to me by her parents was (as I always explained to them, though latterly they always refused to listen) advanced in order that I might invest it for them at my discretion and not as a loan. The trouble was that in those days I had no discretion. Even afterwards when I had the superb idea of the Compactum Dinner, I was not discreet or I should have kept the money that I made. One lives and learns. lam now perfectly discreet. I have written to Wilmay, you will be sorry to hear, to explain myself to her, it is a pity the marriage was broken off." I wrote to thank him, returning his five per cent., and explaining that I was not a money-lender. I also said that there was no necessity on earth for him to write to Wilmay, and I was very sorry he had done it. .

Wilmay's health had seemed to improve with marvellous rapidity at Sinden. Bertha was very hopeful, and in high spirits. She wrote to me to say that Wif may had heard from her uncle, and that she took it quietly, asked a few questions about him, and then seemed not to think about it any more. Wilmay wanted me to come down to Sinden, and Bertha pressed me most warmly to come too. I had been thinking over things, and I had decided what I would do. _ _ _ _

Firstly, I went down to Sinden. Wilmay looked very delicate and sweet; she was less weak than I had expected, and loved to sit out in the sun iv the garden on warm days. I talked to her a little, and then I had a longer talk with the doctors, and I think they told me more than they had told Bertha. Then, when Wilmay was asleep upstairs, and Bertha and I were alone together, I said tc Bertha

"Do you know that I love Wilmay—that I have been in love with her ever since she grew out ol childhood ? "

Bertha paused before she answered. "Once, years ago, I feared it. Then I did not think so any longer." "I believe," I said, "that I shall tell her now." "Yes," said Bertha, quietly. "Tell her." On the next day, when I was sitting in the garden with Wilmay, she spoke of her uncle. "I know what you did for me," she said. "lot wanted to spare me pain and humiliation, and you were going to have paid a thousand pounds just for that little thing. I can't speak about it, but I must thank you." ' "But, Wilmay, as it happens, I did not pay a penny. Let's forget about that man. nothing." "Edward, why have you always been so good tc me? I'm not worth it, and I give ever so mudl trouble." "Do you remember, Wilmay," I said, "that shortly after you came out I went away; for years and did not see you? Do you know why •1 "Why ?" "Because I was growing old, because I was not the man who should marry you, and because I loved you." "Edward! Edward 1" she cried, breathless. _ _

"Don't mind about it, dear; it can't matte: now." " Matter ? It matters all the world. Oh, haven't you seen? I didn't want you to see, but in spite of that—" . " Wilmay, darling, I have always loved you. I love you now." She sighed, and held out her hands to me. l drew her close to me, and looked down into her eyes."

Wilmay said that she was going to be quite well again now. That was terrible for me to hear, who had been made almost sure that she would not be long with us. But for a time it went on—that delusion of growing health and strength. And with all their sadness, these were the happiest months of my life, and Wilmay said that they were the the happiest of her own—happy, though there was in them the solemnity of love and the solemnity of the approaching end. Once only did she speak of Vincent at this time, and once again shortly before her death, when she said that she wanted to know that he had forgiven her. She did know that before she died.

-- -- Of those months I cannot write. They need not words but music.

Before the winter the change came, very sud- denly and very rapidly. A sea voyage was spoken of and abandoned. She knew that she was going to the.

"And let me die quietly," she pleaded, "with Edward near me. We have been away from each other so long." As the end drew near, and she grew weaker and weaker, a change seemed to come over all in the house too, for they all loved her. Customary for- malities and restraints gave way, and there were ghastly mixtures of tragedy and farce. I remember that old Carter broke down once, and apologised for it. Bertha tried to answer him kindly, but without losing her dignity, and then she began to cry too. She found herself taking his hand, for the first time in her life. She died in her sleep. We had the first snow of the winter that night. It fell heavily, and in the morning all the fields were glistening white as if Wilmay . . . If you have ever loved, you will imagine; and *au will know that I cannot write any more.

THE END.

Warrington Lady Speaks Out.

Burns said there are things known only to ourselves which we would not think of communicating even to our most intimate friend. Although Burns was undoubtedly right, there are, nevertheless, occasions when it is our duty to speak out in matters of personal concern. The public weal lays that duty upon us. Miss Esther Ashdown, a well-known and highly res- pected Warrington lady, who resides with her cousin Miss Balker, at 86, Wilderspool-causeway, did not shirk this responsibility, in a conversation a few days ago with a Warrington reporter. About twelve months ago she was alarmed te find that her heath was visibly declining. So complce was the change in her physical condition, that in spite of all a London specialist and the regulation courses of Burgundy, claret, Scc., could Ido she became a physical wreck. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket A draw was the result of Warrington's encounter with Bootle on Saturday. The Bootle contingent were the first to bat and were all got out for 155. When time arrived Warrington had put together 113 for the loss of S wickets and it is probable that if the match had been concluded Bootle would have gained the verdict by a small margin.

Two of the regular players—Messrs E. A. Connor and P. B. Hawtin—were absentees from the Warrington team, and Messrs J. Hallam and F. Barber, the well known footballers, who have:this season been playing for the second eleven, filled the vacancies. The wicket was in a very treacherous condition, and this consider- ably handicapped the players.

J.P. Crouch was Bootle's most successful batsman. When he had complied only seven the Warringtonians made an appeal for catch at wicket, but the referee decided in Crouch's favour.: After this,the batsman hit outlwith alwill and carried out his bat after amassing 51 Other useful contributors were A J Schofield (28, H Williams (18) and J W Jones (13).

It was to Lynes, the Warrington professional, that the saving of the match was mainly due. He ex- hibited a capital defence, and had contributed 35' (not out) when stumps were drawn. The only other War- rington players to attain double figures were Callon (17) and Gibbon (16). Neither Hallam nor Barber had a real chance of showing his abilities with the bat. Barber did not go to the 'thicket at all, whilst Hallam had the misfortune to play on his wickets just when he appeared to be getting in form.

Sandiford displayed good form with the ball for Bootle, capturing 3 wickets for 19 runs.

Cheshire Lines Engineers placed a " feather in their cap" on Saturday, when they defeated Sankey Wire Mills by a majority of 14. Barnes and Bennett were each responsible for 21 for the winners, Bennett being not out. F Williams compiled 35 for the Wire Mills. St. Paul's sustained their first defeat of the season at the hands of the Warrington Weslevans, 9 being the adverse margin Bodle capturedl six wickets for six runs for the victors, and Burton four for seven.

Football.

Leigh R.F.C. have signed on for next season Edward O'Connor, a half-back from Askam, the !Furness club from which they recently recruited a promising wing three-quarter in D L Barr. Twenty-two years of age O'Connor stands 5f t. Bin high and weighs about list. Ile is credited with playing a very smart game behind the scrimmage, and Warrington are stated to have had designs upon him

On Tuesday night a general meeting of the represen- tatives of the Northern League was held at Hudders- field, Mr J. Clifford, Huddersfield, presiding. It was decided that a secretaries' meeting for the arrange- ment of fixtures should be held next Tuesday. The Management Committee was elected as follows :—Mr J Clifford, Huddersfield, president ; Mr A Gresty, Brough- ton Rangers. vice-president ; Mr S Nichol, Halifax, hon treasurer ;Mr J Platt, Oldham, hon. secretary ; and Messrs F Lister' Bradford ; J Goodall, Batley ; R Collins, Rochdale Hornets ; and J B Cooke, Wakefied Trinity.

It will be seen from the above that the Management Committee will consist of five Yorkshire men and three Lancastrians, a i act the significance of which will probably be brought home to some of the Palatine clubs ere the close of the coming season. There is, of course, no reason why such a C mmittee should not be abso- lutely fair, but it would be more satisfactory were the representatives drawn in equal numbers from each of thentwo principal Committees.

Some clubs are already showing commendable activity in their preparations for the campaign. The Leigh club, for instance, is poach ng considerably on our (Warrington's) Askam preserves, and already announces the capture of a promising wing named Barr and a half-back called O'Connor. Like some of Leigh's acquisitions last season they may be no "great shakes," but the fact Is plain that the club is wide- awake. The Hull managers have made a really im- portant haul in tlie persons of thetwo Gloucester half- backs (Goddard and Hall) who have a big reputation in the Rugby Union. Meanwhile lVarrington does not seem to be doing much. It was admitted in the last annual report that new forwards were badly required, and the members are expecting—as they have a right to do, that the demand will be supplied ere the com• mencement of the season and not in the last month. The new rules make forward strength even a more imperative necessity, and a few acquisitions of the right sort are anxiously awaited.

Foot Racing. At the Rochdale Athletic Sports, on Saturday, San- key Harriers and :Rochdale Harriers tied for second place in the two miles inter-club junior race, with 23 points each, and divided the second and third prizes. Straw, of Warrington Harriers, was the first man home.

THE CO-OPERATIVE BOYCOTT. Text of the St. Helens Circular. The reat inlustrial fight at S.: fiele.ns between the newly formed combine of tradesmen and the local co- opentive so.liety, which has nearly 9,000 members, entered on a new and more important phase last night. No one need be reminded the the combine have just rec.ived the approval of counsel to a circular which tney have framed with the idea of influencing the inhabir- ants of eh' town to give their trade to Viten), and not to the Co-operative Society. With the object of laying the draft ot the circular before the general body of trades- men, a meeting was held at the Fleece Hotel last night, when the proceedings were private so far as the Pres were concerned. _

There were about 300 shopkeepers present, represent- ing various branches of trade, and the meeting was most enthusiastic throughout, the speeches being punctuate at very frequent intervals with loud cheers. Councillor John Green, the chairman, in the coure of his remarks, challengel the Co-operative society to fight them by giving up dividends and cutting prices. There was much discussion afterwards, and the meet- ing adopte.l the circular unanimously. _ . . . _

The circular is in the following terms "At a recent meeting convened by the St Helens and D:stric'; Grocers and Provision Heelers' Association, pre- sided over by their cheirtnio,Mr Counciller Jelin Green, J.".,and embracing not only members of the Association named, but a large number of other Sc Helens residents engaged or interested in private, as distinguished from co-operative, trading, the question, of the local relation- ship between these two classes of enterprise and the persons eogaged in them and directly and indirectly related to mem was the subject of very careful con- -5j leration. "The gross injustice in the incidence of the competition etween private traders and co-operative committee was clearly demonstrated. The members of the former class have to find their own capital, and have to pay income tax, while the latter oh sin their capital from their customers, and are exempt from income-tax. " The openly declared purpose of co-operation is to exterminate the private trader ; the process is in toler- ably full swing in St Helens ; and the persons present at the recent meeting felt that the time had come for meeting combined action on the otLier side with com- bined action on ours. " It was accordingly resolved to be for the good of the town and its legitimate trade that those interested therein should do all in their power to reduce the influ- unties of the local co-operative society,end endeavour to attract as rainy of its present friends as possibli to a healthier way of thinking. No private trader or friend of private trade should ' either ~Lipport the stores or anyone who acts for or with te stores. Clergymen, professional men, bankers, mister builders, joiners, plumbers, pa.intere, printers, saddlers, wheelwrights, smitbe, agents, and shopmen have all in the past been dependent on one another, and had freedom to pursue their individual callings for their pqsonal profit ; and any who now even patronise ce-operation are helping to forge fetters for themselves and others, the weight of which may soon be painfully felt. •• It is earnestly hoped that they will actively identify themselves with this policy of effective self-defence. Support no one who t 3 supporting in any degree or accept support from any organisation which may sooner or later prove a social ani flea icial rata to the town." During the proceedings this telegram was road from the Stalybridge Grocers' Association :—" Accept my hearty congratulations in your example to the United Kingdom. Hope to follow suit. E Stamp, president." As for the attitude of the Co-operative Society, the management seem to look upon the movement with complacency, and are confident that their members will remain loyal to them. Up to the present they have not had much to say on the matter, but it is assumed in the town that when the tradesmen's circular has been issued they may consider it advantageous to issue some sort of counterblast. _ .

Shoalg of letters have been received from all parts of the country by the tradesmen's leaders.

Coroner in a Fix.

Mr S Brighouse, County -Ca—roner, was to have held an important inquest at Glazebury,near Leigh, on Tuesday evening, but on his arrival he announc- ed to the waiting jurymen, Press, and police that he was unablt to do so on account of the failure to turn up of his clerk•—The disappearance, he ex- plained, was most mysterious, inasmuch as he saw his clerk on the platform of Lime-street Station, Liverpool, a few minutes before stepping into the train. As the missing gentleman had charge of all the papers, he (the Coroner) had no inquisition,pre- cept, or burial order. Eventually the inquest had to stand adjourned situ Wednesday.

GOODS TRAFFIC ON TRAMWAYS.

The Cheapening of Transit.

mportant South Lancashire Scheme.

A meeting cf the members of the Liverpool Cnamber of Commerce was held on Monday aftemoon, at the Exchange Station Hetet, for the purpose of hearing a paper by Mr J E Waller, al I C E, ef London, en " Through tea() on tramways for passengers and goods " In the absence of the president, Flu' Alfred Jones, the chair was occupie.l by Mr G H Cox ; and the Sttentiance also included Sir John whicoir. NI F., Messrs C Lancaster. Alfred Holt, E K Muspratt, J E Rayner, H A Watson, Vose (Bolton Chamber of Commerce), E Shrapnel Smith (general manager, Bold Carrying Com- pany), J Gardner, Mentagu Big oieson, 0 E Parker, J B 1 Atherton, Jacob Atherton, D A Davies, J A Marsh, Alfred Tracey, OP Proctor, W T Millett, Douglas, T Upton, William MtNaught, W D -telt, W a Atty., D M Gaskin, John Wesen, J K Slater, and Thomas H Barker (secretary). I The Chairman, in introducing Mr Waller, expressed I the opinion that the cheapsning of inland means of , transit was of greater importance to the manufacturers of this country than the question cf t wiffs and subsidies (Hear, hear.) If we bad the coeap transit of the I United States, Germany, France, and Belgium, bc,h by rail and canal, we would be able to compote on better terms than our compeoitors for the international trade of the world. A significant review appeared in that day's "Daily Post "of a report jusc presented by the Iron Trade Association, emphasising the clearanese of railway treight in the States, and when they considered that in this country many of the canals were in the hands of the railway companies, and that on the Conti- nent new canals, which were to be free of toil, were be- ing constantly made, they must see the importrinee of considering any means of cheapening inland traffic: in this couatry. Mr. Waller commenced his poet with a reference to the recent development of electric trams or light rail- ways, resulting in some cases to tbe provision of an ' extensive and connected system, and presenting possibilities not only of through passenger cars, but also of introducing goods traffic. In order to' obtain the full advantages rendered possible by Me linking uo of tram- way systems, it was essential that " throueh " running facilities should be granted by the various authorities owning the tramways physically connected and to the same gauge. This necessity had long since been recog- nised in respect to railways, and it would be ludicrous to propose that when there is a " through " system of tramways constructed between, say, Leigh and the docks in Liverpool that a passenger who desired to go through the whole length by tr tracer should have, not- withstanding the physical connection of the metals throughout that length, to travel in no less than four different cars because the four different sections over I which he travelled were in the separate ownership of 1 the South Lane ishire Tramways Company, the St. Helens Corperation, the Lancashire Light Rill- ways Company, and the Corporation of Liverpool. It was certain that numbers of people would be glad to avail Merits •Ivea of the tramways in preference if they bad the opportunity. It was true that the trams could not compete with the railways in speed, but the service would be more accessible and more frequent. The construction of the South Lancashire tramways, together with the Liverpool and Prescot systems, would represent an aggregate length of nearly 400 miles andlithe question of goods traffic between the various towns which would be connected was of vital importance. In the South Lancashire district there was an enormous goods traffic. The parely local traffic consisted principally of coal, bricks, building meteri- ale, hardware and machinery, of which an immense amment,representing many thousands of tons per annum was at present carted, principally coal, from collieries to mills and brickfields, and the return traffic in bricks to the collieries. The traffic it was impossible for the railway companies to deal with, as the mills and brick- fields were not connected with the railways by sidings, and even in the case of collieries so cos- neoted such sidings were valueless as regards local traffic, as while the cartage to the transmitting station would be avoided, it would still become necessary to cart from the receiving station to the point of destination. It was found that, the ccst of double handling and caging thus involved, over and above the railway rate, rendered a single asrtage from works to works cheaper aq well as more cone enient and expeditious: The through traffic to the district in gee I- tion from Liverpool, Manchester, and Warrington covered nearly every conceivable class of goods, includ- ing raw cotton, cotton manufactured goods, grain, flour, provisions, ordinary and mining timber, iron and steel manufactures, glass, alkali, earthenware, acid, oil, grease, tallow and manure. Of this through traffic the bulk was, of coarse, handled by the railway com- panies, although a very considerable amount was coo- yeyed by canal and by cart, A large traffic in raw cotton was' dealt with by the latter means, both from Liverpool and Manchester, as the rates were cheaper, excessive handling and transhipment was av Aded, and She delivery was more expeditious; Traffic sent by rail, unless in truck loads, inevitably met with delay by reason of the transhipment involved at junction stations and considerable damage frequently arises by reason of so much handling. The existence of this considerable road traffic at!,the present time indicated pretty clearly that, if a Cheaper and more expeditious service for goods onithe tramways ' was oroanized, a very large traffic will be available,and, if the authorities owning the various tramway systems were prepared to give the requisite running facil ties on fair and equitable terms, there appeared to be absolutely no difficulty is organising such a service. Sack traffic, properly controlled, should prove of no inconvenience to the sky of Liverpool, but a source of profit to the tram- way undertaking of the Corporation, a convenience to the ordinary traffic during the day time—to the extent to which it removed this goods traffic off the streets— and, to some extent, a possible increase in the traffic of the port if facilities are given for the conveyance from Liverpool of goods now entering the district through o her channels. A depot or depots, conveniently situ- ated in close proximity to the tramways, would have to be estaolished, so that a siding mighe be laid in from these tramwave to the depot. AS a general practice, flat-decked trucks would appear most suitable for the conveyance of the whole of the goods traffic. Each such truck or waggon would be provided with detachable tabs or crates on rollers, in plasm of the ordinary box body, being somewhat similar to the arrangement at present in use by several of the South Lancashire collieries for the purpose of facilitating the transport of coal. This type of goods waggon was also similar to that adopted on the Donegal Railway, where the 1 general manager (Mr R fl Livesay) bad had I to contend with the question of transit over I two different gauges. Mr Livesay's description of this system was as follows :—" No lifting arrangements are required, as the bodies are taken over by means of Tonere, which tun on rails secured to the underframes. We carry any description or class of goods in them: The system was only brought hate use about four years ago, and since them it has been such a success that we have decided to gradually alter the whole of our goods &en wageons to it; it does away with delays else to transnipment and lass through breakage, besides effecting a great saving in the cost of handling, as two men can do all that is required in a few seconds." The conditions of working on a tramways system would, however, be somewhat different to those existing on the Donegal Railway. There it is only necessary to move the truck body in its entirety from one deck waggon to another,but the fact that the body would, in the case of a tramway service, have to be moved from a street Miry to a tramway waggon, and vice versa, involved the necessity of the body being constructed in sections, as previously suggested, the size of these sections being controlled by the s'ze which could conveniently be carried, on the ordinary turtles in use in the district served. Proper provision would have to be made for eecuring the sections in position on the truck or under- frame during transit. The same fiat-decked waggons with the crates or bodies removed would also be avail- able for the carriage of other classes of goods, such as , timber, girders, portable engines, machinery, and boilers, the deck being provided with rings for roping- ' in gee Is and with sockets for bolsters and Stanchions ' for such traffic as timber. It was not suggested that all ' classes of traffic could be dealt with by this description ' of waggon. There were other classes of traffic for seloich ordinary wageons would be more suitable. For instance in the event of traffic arising from collieries in , the South Lancashire district, where the coal bad to be conveyed 'direct to the docks for shipping porpoises, ordinary waggons would probably be needed. which , wool I run direct on to the tipping cradles at the docks. Such traffic as this, however, was somewhat apart from the question now under consideration' which relates to . the method of avoiding unnecessary handling in tran- shipment where there was no possibility of direct com- munication between the point at which the traffic ori- ginates and its destination. It was proposed, where- ever practicable, to lay sidings into the larger collieries. works, and depots alone the tramway routes ; and al- ready a number of probable'customers bad signified their desire to have such sidings connected with their estab- lishments. Much local traffic between collieries, mills, and brickfis les would thus be dealt with without any break of bulk whatever and without the use of the special type nf waggons suitable for transhipment, but to accommodate through traffic, or traffic originating at, say, a colliery in the distr;ct which bad to be distri- buted in comparatively small consignments to general traders and the public, not connected by sidings, it would be necessary to provide suitable depots at con- venient points on me tramway system. The depots of the company, whether in proximity to the Liverpool decks or at any point on the company's system, would all be worked in a similar manner, and the process I pro- posed might be illustrated by taking the case of goods coming to Liverpool by sea for delivery within the die- triet. Such goods would be loaded from the quays or- dock warehouses on to an ordinary road lurry in accord

ance with the present practice,excePting,that instead of being placed direct en the lurry,the lurry would be pro- vided with one or more of the company's crates or detachable bodies, and the goods where practicable w mid be packed in such crate. The lurry would then proceed alongside the stage or platform at the receiving depot, be rolled off bv means of rollers on to such stage, whence it would be transferred to a deck-waggon on an adjoining siding. A train of these waggous would then be in readiness to proceed on its journey during the following night-time. Ia the event of the goods being consigned to premises connected with the tramways by siding no further handling would be involved, but in other cases precisely the reverse process would be gone through at the nearest depot to the consignee' p.emises. The handling in either case would be con-s aicierably less than in the case of railway carriage as from the time of the first loading into the crates there would be no interference with or tranship- ping of goods until they were put out either on the depot stage or into the consignee's siding at the point of destination, and bulk would never b, breken except in the case of extremely small consignment!. At the present time in the case of goods to be conveyed by rail from the docks at Liverpool no less than six tranship- i ments were involved,even in cases were full truck loads could be forwarded, avoiding intermediate banding at junction stations:—(l)Loading at warehouse into lurries; (2) 'reloading therefrom to stage at railway company's depot; (3) loading from stage to trucks (to say nothing ,of extra loading and unioadiog wheat seat coronae-1 a ! depot without rail connectioel : (4) uelcaling trucks to depot stage at destination ; (5) loading from stage to lurry; and (6) unloading from lorry at csnsignee'a premisea. The trsm cage, while creating and dealing with a special traffic of their own and giving faCilities ' for. the delivery of goods al traders along the route of the tramways, would probably beemas before long re- ; c )gnised distributors and feeders to the railway systems, as the conservatism of the railway companies could not 1 long withstand itilloV atloOs of proved practicability. A very large traffic could be dealt with, and the carrying capacity of t'le lines, if, and when foiled necessary, be largely augmented by increasing the amount of double lines throughout the system. Apart from the goods traffic proper on the .tramways, [there doubtless would be a considerable par- cels traffics. Such paresis traffic would probably be car:ied on in the daytime, either by trailer cars attached to paasenor cars, or by special independent self-propelled parcels vans. The question of cost of carriage by tramway was one which had received care- ful consideration,and though it is obviously impracticable and inconvenient in the present paper to give schedules of rates at which the varinus classes of goods could be carried, it might safely be stated that the traffic could be conveyed at a cost considerably less than was at present Involved. (Appiausa) Mr J E Rayner asked how Mr Waller proposed to pro- vide storage accommodation for, say, a thousand tons of noalscoming to Llverool. Mr Waller Fad they must provide sidings just as the railway companies bad done—probably out at Knotty Ash, whore land was cheaper—and run the traffic through quickly on the following night. The advent tee the tramways would give was the avoidance of tranship- ment, and the better handling of local traffic, which was the only traffic they would deal with. Mr Alfred Holt moved a vote of thanks to Mr Waller, though of opinion that he had suggested no remedy for the difficulties of inland carriage. He did not think that transhipment would be re lucel, because it was impos- sible to load from the steamer to wagger:re on rails. The only solution, in his oninion, was the plateway system. Mr C E Parker (Warringten), in seconding, said he thought Mr Waller bad made out a very strong case for inquiry. The resolution was passed and acknowledged by Mr Waller.

There was a very large congregation et the morning service, when the sermon was preached by the Lord Bishop of Liver- prl from the words," Woo is this that cometh leaning on the arm of her belovsd ?" The church was again crowded at Evensong, when the preacher was the Rev. H. Alden Nash, vicar of St. Mark's, Liverpool. The services were conducted be the Vicar on both occasione. The singing was of a hearty description, the members of the choir acquitting themselves creditably. The morning anthem was " 0, worship the King," ny J Fl Maunder, while in the eveaing Simper's " Give Glory unto the Lord " was sung, the tenor solo belay, sweetly rendered by M.. W. N. Grounds. Tee Magnificat and Norm Dimittis were to Adams in G. Mr. Blackwell pre- ided at the organ.

Warrington Steamer Wrecked.

A telegram from Amlwebport on Tuesday stated that the steamer Sarah Brough, from Dublin to Llanddulas, light:, struck on the Platters Rocks on Monday night, and sank in deep water. The crew were saved in their boats. The Sarah Brough was a vessel of 299 tons gross, and belonged to W A Savage, Liverpool-road, Warrington. Horticultural Show at Oughtrington,

The fifth annual exhibition of the Oughtrington Floral and Horticultural Society was held in Oughtrington Park, by permission of Mr G Powys Dewhurst, on Saturday afternoon, and was visited by a large number of people. The judges were Messrs Plant (Altrinchatn) and Smith (Arley Hall), who had a specially difficult task in judging the entries for the best kept cottage flower and veget- able gardens. Some artistic gardens were visited in Lymm. Thelwall, High Te.gh, &c., and where all were so good it was a difficult matter to make the final awards, The judges also had much praise for the boys' allotments,which were above the average. An attractive programme of sports was gone through. After the prize-giving Mr John R Newton (Warburton) asked the large crowd to give a hearty vote of thanks to Mr and Mrs G P Dewhurst not only for the work they had done for the show but also for the use of the beautiful gardens and park. In response Mr Dewhurst, on behalf of his wife and self, thanked Mr Newton and those present for their kindness and hoped that another year they would have the prizes spread over a ;large number of exhibitors. He would like to see the prize,s carried off by a large number of cottagers, and he could assure them that all would receive fair treat- ment.

"WARRINGTON-THE HALFWAY HOUSE."

Interesting Retrospect. An illustrated guide bas just been issued by the Ches- hire Lines Comm.ttee, entitled "New Routes and Old Acres." It Las been prepared by Mr T E Edwardes, editor of " The Tourist," and Contains a mass of inter- Enting local information, copiously Illustrated. We extract the following from chapter seven entitled "War- rha gton—the halfway house." TRAVEL WAS DIFFERENT THEN.

A local authority is responsible for the statement that " down to the middle of the eighteenth century there was no highway into Liverpool suitable for carriages, and it was necessary co jiurney to Werrington on horse- back before a stage coach or public conveyance could be met with for c irnmunication with London and other parts of the country," and that " it was not till 1780 that the first regul sr stage coach ran between Liverpool and London." Through Wa,rington, built on the &Utley runs the Old Coach-road from Liverpool to London. Nay, Warrington was the starting point of the first stage coach which tamed a wheel in England. How this unique bit of history sends the mind agog with thought !

PRESENT DAY CONTRASTS.

The great facilities which Warrington enjoys for the transit of goods at roasonab'e rates both by rail may and canal, i:s central pisitioa bet wen Liverpoll and Man. cheater, together with its command of Irish and Scotch markets through the pprt of Liverpoo:, gives it substan- tial advantages ; and now t.) these benefits has to be added a new trad. by means of the M incheater Ship Canal.

WHEN THE DOMESDAY SURVEY WAS. Warr n3;ton is a really ancient place. At the Circe of the Domesday Survey it was called Waliotune, and was then the head of the hundred,but now merged into that of West Derby, the lord of the manor being the Marquis of Salisbury, KG. At the period of Eiward 111 (1351) a deed, still preserved, gives the name of the town as Werynton. To-day Warrington is a place of nearly 70,000 inhabitants, and the seat of industrial and manu- facturing businesses, many oZ which continually in- crease almost by leaps and bounds. Among the warner- 'ooB industries of the place are wire drawing, the manu- facture of iron in bar, hoops, wire rods, a 3 well as of tools and files, cut glass, and soap, and it has long been celebrate' for its piano wires, which have an European reputation. There are large and extensive f,r2es, iron- foundries, breweries, chemical works, and mills ; it is also the centre of the northern tanning trade for heavy sole leather—a great part of the leather used in the b ,ots and shoes manufactured for the army and police being made in the neighbourhood. Other works there are besides, viz.: pins, b4rax, table and decorative glass, bedsteads, rubber, gas stoves and heating apparatus : articles made from wire ; soap, glycerine,and scent,tte. Thus Warrington does a big goods trade with the Cheshire Lines. SOME MUNICIPAL POSSESSIONS.

The Town Hall, in Stukey•street, form illy known as Bank Hall, was purchased from Lord Winmarleigh by the Corporation, in 1872, for the sum of £22,000. This amount included the cost of the grounds (18i acres) in which the Hall stands, and which the Corporation has converted into a public park. The building, which was erected in 1750 from the designs of the famous archite Leon!, is substantially built in the classic style, and commands an extensive v'ew over one of the most beautiful districts in Cheshire. The Museum, erected in 1855-7, contains library, museum, art gallery, and lec- ture room. The Museum proper contains a fine collec- tion of shells, birds, fossils, minerals, old coins, di-e. Local antiquities are well represented, and include remains from the R3man station at Wildersoool and the Austin Friary Church lately discevered. In the Art Gallery is the great marble group of " Webs& and Satan," by J. Warrington Wood. a native of the place. The Market Hall, erected in 1156, is a covered building near the centre of the place. Wednesday is the princi- pal trading day for butter, eggs, and vegetables. A corn market is also held each Wednesday, Saturday being also another market day. 'REGARDING SO3SE OLD ACRES.

Near Bewsey all and Winwick. on the old Roman road between Warrington and Wigan, is a reputed battlefield of Cromwell's. Here he is said to have dis- persed and dismayed a fairly large number of R wallets who had rallied attrr the still greater defeat at Preston. History spoke, though, at Wa. rington long before the Proctor'., time. There was a Rom4n encuripment between Wilderspool and Stockton Heath (Warrington), and some of the roads diverging therefrom are supposed to have been constructed in the first century. Here, from time to time, ancient coins and curious antiquarian fragments hive been found, and, as mentioned above, preserved. The Warrington Clergy Orphan Institution occupies the site of a large mound known as the Mote Hill. Here was a house of Augustine Friars, founded before 1279, and dissolvAl by that perhaps not ill-mean- ing but still arch enemy to such religious institutions, Henry VIII. The memory of the building is still pre- served in the names of Friars' Gate and Friars' Green. The site of the church was discivered and the greater part of the foundations of its walls laid bare in 1886, when the tile pavement of the choir and a number of ca,ved stones were removed and placed in the M1190111:12. An inscription was at the same time placed on a build- ing in Friars' Green, which occupies a portion of the site of the nave. A good specimen of a room of the 13th century may still be seen in Fennel-street, and there are black-and-white timbered houses of the 17th century in the Market Place, Buttermarket-street, and Church-tree. AND SOME MATTERS ECCLESIASTICAL.

The Church of St Elphin occupies tbe site of an ancient Norman structure erected in the 12% century. and repliced by a cruciform buildiag in the clecJrated style. In 1824 a small door leading to a stone stairway, con- ducting to a crypt under the chancel, was discovered on the left side of the chancel, and on the steps was

- found a chasuble richly embroidered with figures of various saints. This vestmeut was ultimately given to the Rev Dr Molvneux, Catholic Priest of St Alban's, Warrington, and is now part of the furniture of that church. The Church of St Elphin is well worthy of a visit on account of its numorous stained glass windows and memorials Gf brass to the Lyons and Blackburne families. There is also in the Bewsey Chapel the altar tomb of alabaster of Sir John Boteler, Knight, who was murdered at Bewsey in 1643 Emirs FOR TRAVELLERS AND 01TIERS. There are several good hotels in Warrington which cater not only for cyclists but for a snorting and sight- hying public.
