A SUNDAY AFTERNOON SCENE IN IIdILL-LANEI—ALLEGATION3

A.QA.I.Iff4T CONSTAELE.

At the Wartington Police Court on Monday. before Mr F Monks (presiding) and other magistrates, Joseph Salt, 2. Mill-lane, was charged with assaulting P 0 Clough whilst acting in the execution of his duty in Mill lane at 340 on Sunday afternoon.—Mr Browne appeared for the defendant. P C Clough stated that at the time ha question he had the defendant's son in custody and was trying to get his name and address for shooting atones from a catapult. Defendant came up, together with some other relatives of the boy, and prevented him from getting the name and address. Defend- ant then tried to take the boy in the house, but witness would not let him. He told defendant that he was a police officer, bat 'defendant yr-Wetly struck him a violent blow on the side of the head His neck had been painful ever since. P.O 's Newey and Gibbs came on the scene and took defendant to the Police Station. Witness was Obliged I tl take the boy to the Potice Station to obtain his name and I address. Defendant was subsequently bailed out.--Cross- examined by Mr Browne, witness stated that the boy broke the catapult whilst witness was chasing him. The boy did not give his name and address. He did not know that the lad was suffering from an injury to his arm and that he was screaming with pain because he (Clough) was grasping it so tightly. He did not threaten to hit Mr Salt with his cane. The defendant did not push witness to prevent him from steiking him kdefendant), but wilfully struck him with his clenched fiat. By the Chief Constable : Witness had re- ceived a communication about the use uf catapults in that district from Mr Taylor, Police Court Missionary, Witness was frequently interfered with in this manner when he had occasion to arrest boys. He heard the defendant say at the Police Station that he did not know witness was a police- officer, or he would not have assaulted him.—P.O. Newey stated that Clough was surrounded by a large crowd. It was worse than Town's.encl." Defendant rushed out of the house and't punched " Clough in the face.—Mr ii Taylor. P.C.M , said he made a complaint to Clough about three boys catapulting in Piekmere-street. He did not know whether defendant's son was one of them —Superintendent Monaghan said he saw defendant at the Police Station and he stated that he did not know Clough was a police officer or he would not have struck him. He thought the constable was a farmer.—By Mr Browns: Defendant was in custody from four until nearly ten o'elock.—For the defence Mr Browne sal i defendant was a man of tha highest respecta- bility and had lived in Warrington 27 years. There was nothing whatever against him. On Sunday afternoon he was washing himself and heard a commotion in the street. Re ran out and saw his son being held by a man whom he thodght was a farm labourer from Sankey Hall. The boy's arm was Talented by an aceident a year or two ago and only a week ago had to be re-bifida:ad and embrocation applied. Clough was holding the boy by the arm, and the boy was screaming. Defendant went to Clough, not knowing that he was a policeman, and told him to let the boy g and summon him. The bay gave his name. The constable was going to strike the defendant with a stick and Mr Salt said ''lf you dare to hit me, I will bit you beak." Clough raised tbe stick a second time and defendant pushed him in the face. He (Mr Browne) submitted that the boy was wrongfully in custody. He could not be charged with discharging a missile from an instrument under the Towns Police Clauses Act when that Instrument was not in order. And if Clough did not see him commit an offence the boy was Illegally in custody. How, therefore. could Mr Salt be charged with assaulting a constable in the execution of his duty when the boy had committed no offence for which he could be taken into custody ?—P.O Clough was recalled and stated that the bag of stones produced was taken from the boy's pocket. He saw the boy shooting with the catapult.—The chairman said that on a point of law the Bench would decide against Mr Browne. After the complaints of catapulting, the police had a perfect right t 3 take a boy whom they suspected into custody, and no one had any right to interfere. It was for the Court to decide whether the police had done their duty or not.—Defendant then gave evidence and said Clough neverialtold him he was a policeman. Witness was unable to eleneh his fist. —Elizabeth Millington. Green-street. corroborated defend- ant's version of the affair.—Mrs Beechall, B. Lloyd-street, said Clough struck the boy several times. The boy gave his name and address when in Lloyd-street.—Mrs Burns, 34, Lloyd-etreet, said Clough handled the boy roughly and struck him. Witness spoke to him about it and he replied " I I shall strike you." The boy gave his name and address.—The Bench considered their decision in private, and on their return into Court the Chairman said the Benca looked to I ' every respectable citizm—and the believed the defendant to be one—to support the ponce in the execution of their duty. While they thought that, perhaps, the defendant had gone a little in excess of what he should have done, they were disposed to give him the benefit of the doubt and dis- miss the case, feeling that possibly in the excitement of the moment he might not have heard Clough Say that he was a policeman. HABITUAL THIEF AT WARRINGTON—SINGULAR DIFFCULTY. A boy named John Griffin, 37, Seotland-road, was brought before the Warrington magistrates on Saturday,and charged with stealing six brass oil cups, value 303, from the black- smith's shelp of Edward Connor on Thursdal evening.—The Chief Constableleald that on'Thureday the prisoner went to Mr Samuel ?Milton, Allen-street, and offered the oups for sale stating that hie uncle had sent him with them, Mr Plinston was suspleious that the boy had not ;mune by them honestly and in order that the cups might not be take's away and thus I lost sight of he made a formal purchase of them for 2 and immediately informed the pollee. Prisoner was arrested by Superintendent Monaghan and P C dough. When charged he made a number of contradictory statements, and finally admitted stealing the articles from Mr Connor's yard. Mr Plinston's action in the matter was perfectly boitelide, because Mr Connor had not evea missed the cups when Mr Plinston gave information. Prisoner was one of a gang of young thieves which the magistrates and police were making smaller by degrees. The lads in the gang were inveterate and habitual thieves—making thieving a daily practice. In May last prisoner was imprisoned for three days for theft, and in December of last year he was dealt with under the First Offenders' Ack for stealing by means of trick,—lt was stated that defendant was between 14 and IS years of age, and the magistrates ordered him to he sent to a reformatory until he was 19.—0 n Monday morning prisoner again appeared in the dock,and the Chief Constable explained that after the Court had risen on Saturday it was found that prisoner was over 17 years of age, and the reformatory authorities would, of course, refuse to receive him. The Clerk advised that the only thing that could be done was to discharge the lad. It was a most extraordinary case, and they knew what Dickens said about the law.—The Chairman, Tbe law is " a bass." (Laughter.)—lt was decided to send the lad to a reforma,ory as previously arranged, and if the authorities refused to admit him the Chief Constable was instructed by the magistrates to discharge him.

Personal.

Much anxiety has been fait with respect to Lord Gerard's condition, in view of the reports circu- lated. Lady Gerard has sent the following tele- gram regarding her husband's condition to Gars- wood Hall :—" The report in the papers incorrect. His lordship is in the sarat condition as he has been for the last ten days.—llary Gerard." _

, . Lord Gerard, whose illness at Garswood, Lanca- shire, has been perhaps too alarmingly reported, is, like his father before him, the head of the Lanca- shire Hussars, one of the finest Yeomanry carps in the North, the nucleus of which came from the I Gerard estates. The regiment was honourably rep- resented in the war; while L3rd Gerard, one of the earliest volunteers for the campaign, served on Sir Redvar's Butler's staff, his activities gaining for I him a " mention " from his chief. Sir Redvers, soast after his return from South Africa, was Lord and Lady Gerard's guest at Garswood. the old Lancashire home of the family, where the King also stayed in the early nineties. Lord Gerard, who red by Disraeli orperhap as a ns endowed the " Garswood Bed "in the imperial Yeomanry Hospital, is the premier Roman Catholic baronet; but, though he claims lineage from Otho, a great lord of Alfred's time, he is only the second o baron.item iTnh he is peeragepolipolicy y f conciliation,nfer was co " education '"'of the Gerard& co-religionists.

HENRY MILES AND CO.'S ADVE ansEm ENT CONTINUED.

TIIE departure of The New Chancellor. LORD SALISBURY is bj

no means the most im- portant change which awaits the Cabinet. Since _ .

his cesignation of the Foreign Secretaryship he has done none of the detailed work of administra-

tion. He never took the business of supervising his subordinates seriously. The old ideal was that the Premier should see each of his Ministers daily. That was impossible in a Cabinet of twentj and an aged man at its head. Moreover, while , the Ministry includes two nephews, a son, and a

son-in-law, LORD SALISBURY has retired only in the most formal sense of the word. He is still the head of the House of Cecil, which is still the dora- Mating element in the Government. The approach- ing resignation of Sir MICEIARL HICKS-BEACIIIS in reality an event of greater consequence. For iP a Ministry which tended to fall into two familY groups he was a solitary and an independent h'° a very powful force. He leaves no successor, and his ideas are represented only by a few thoughtful Tories, who are not at all likely to be called t° the front bench. Eveats had been too much for him in the last few years, but to the best of hit.

individual strength he had combated the grov/log tendency to Imperialism and extravagance. -FILS Budget swelled from year to year, but no one wio more alarmed than he at its monstrous growth,and no one warned the nation more faithfully against the tide which bore him along. With his retire- ment the new tendencies will reign unchecked.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
_-- -,,,, isiarili -- - -_--_ -

There will be no one to resist the reckless demands for doles and armaments, no one to preach the unpopular doctrine of retrenchment, no one to stand stoutly in the breach which has already been made in our traditional system of Free Trade. Probably because,he knew that his own resignation would not be long postponed—it is no secret that for two years past he has remained at his post only from personal loyalty to Lord SALISBURY—he surrendered too easily to his Protectionist col- leagues. The Bread Tax has paved the way for the changes that are now inevitable. Mr. CHAM- BERLAIN has long been an open advocate of some Imperial system of Protection. If he has failed so far to put his theories into practice, that was only because nothing could be done while the CHAN- CELLOR of the EXCHEQUER remained a convinced Free Trader. But la his last great speech at Bir- mingham Mr. CHAMBERLAIN ;threw all disguises aside. That manifesto is now of greater import- ance than on the day it was uttered. It told, as plainly as words can speak, that in the view of the KING'S most powerful minister Free Trade is an "obsolete shibboleth," that Imperial unity can be attained and a commercial supremacy secured only by the abandonment of the system which under- lies the prosperity and the growth ofihalf a century.. No doubt the change will be cautiously prepared. We shall be told that any sacrifice is worth making which may lead to Free Trade within the Empire. No great revolution will be attempted at first. It will seem a trivial concession to allow a prefer- ence to a few Colonial products such as Australian wines. But a beginning of this kind is fat 0. One cannot grant favours to Australia and withhold -them from Canada. One cannot allow a rebat on wine and refuse it on wheat or sugar. And A the Canadian wheat-grower or the Victorian vint- ner are assisted, by what logic could we refuse to help the timber-mercl ant of Ontario or the sheep- farmer of New Zealand P We may continuo to import impartially from all the world, convinced that our interest is to obtain cheap food wherever we can buy it to the best advantage. Or we may decide to 'sacrifice our own poorer classes in order to permit the Colonial producer to charge high prices and develop his own resources at our expense. But in the long run we shall have to follow one system or the other with some approach to consistency.

IT will be a very interest- The Czar and ing sequel to the Hague " Trusts." Conference if the CZAR

should succeed in carrying out his present inten- tion of calling an International Conference to make war on "Trusts" and "Combines." These organisations are fast becoming the common scourge of all civilised peoples. Their operations extend far outside the frontiers of the States in which they originate, and if they can be fought

it is obvious that there must be some cohesion among their victims. But it is hard to guess what means can be devised. The Americans are an ingenious and an inventive people. They have long been at the mercy of trusts which control their railways, their coal, their steel and their o From day to day we read how one groat piratical clique is making an assault on their beef supply while another attacks their wheat,so that between

_ the two something like famine threatens New York, and riots have been of almost daily occur- rence. For years past one of their two great American parties has bent all its energies to com- bat these Trusts by legislation. It has not suc- ceeded, and one may well doubt whether the united wisdom of Europe could achieve much liksliz where the acute wits of the American democracy

_ have conspicuously failed. There is, however, one condition which is almost indispensable to the growth of a "Combine "—a high protective tariff. No doubt even with Free Trade " trusts" are possible. We have them in this country in the electrical industry, in the manufacture of thread and of tobacco, for example. But so long as our ports are open to foreign merchandise, so long as competition in our markets is unrestricted, no syndicate can ever attain ths tyrannous strength of the American Trusts, because it cannot become a genuine monopoly. It is only since the McKin- ley tariff practically closed the ports of the United States to European imports that the Trusts have grown so powerful and so selfish. They can fix their own prices so long as they control the legislature and idictate the tariff. To achieve political influence is thus one of the first objects of a Trust's ambition in any Protec- tionist country. Thus we find in South Africa that as soon as the group of capitalists—of which the ECKSTEINS and Mr. Barr are the chief—have bent the Government to their will and destroyed the Dutch Republics, they at once start the manu- facture of dynamite, because they can rely on Lord MILNER to impose duty of ss. per case on the im- ported article. That is a useful object-lesson. Monopolies flourish under Protection, and are secure only when they can control the Gov ern- meat. The moral is obvious. If we cannot destroy " Trusts," at least we can refrain from creating the condition under which alone they can become a menace. :Pres Trade and unrestricted competition are our best safeguards. If, fo example, we were to adopt Mr. CHAMBERLAIN' Zollverein—cenfine our wheat supply to Canada, our mutton to New Zealand, and our sugar to Jamaica —we should very soon find that all these articles would be" cornered," and we should corn to depend for our daily broad o a the tender mercie of half-a-dozen foreign Jews. Wheat is safe a present only because Canada, Russia, and the United States compete freely in our markets,

Sir Edward Grey's motion on Sir Radversßaller's behalf was defeated by a very heavy majority' and the voting by no means, followed ordinary party lines. General Buller s friends have cer- tainly succeeded in exposing the inconsistency and Weakness of the Government. In fact, the more we have come to know about this mysterious case the less it is possible to approve the conduct of any of the parties. One cannot rate Sir Redvers Buller's personal qualities too highly—the courage which won him the V.C., his care for the lives of i his men,his mercy to friend and foe, his success n the management oF the commissariat, and, rarest quality of all, the bluff honesty which showed so well in his refusal to re-write his own despatches at the bidding of the War Office. These are valuable and, above all, able qualities. But a general must have something more. At Colenso General Buller failed disastrously, and it is hard to see why he was treated so much More leniently than General Gatacre. The panic in which he incited General White to surrender Ladysmith was even more inexcusable. The same weakness appeared at Spion Kop, where, from a kindly desire not to interfere with Sir Charles Warren, he allowed his plans to be muddled, with calamitous results. After repeated mistakes of this kind a strong Government would have put a more capable General in command. Instead of this Sir Redvers was left in the field, thanked for his exertions, and even promoted to the command of the First Army Corps. So far from showing

animus against him the War Offi co behaved with excessive deference towards a deservedly popular commander, presumably because it lacked the courage to do its duty. In the end, after passing over his really serious mistakes, it punished him severely for a trivial breach of etiquette. The story of the garbled despatches makes ugly reading, but worst of all is the revelation of the weakness and hesitation which left an important command at a critical moment in the hands of a roan whom the democracy idolised, while all the while the War Office had in its pigeon-holes the clearest evidence that he was unequal to his great responsibility.

The success which attended the cycle parade of two years ago has emboldened the members of the Warrington Cycling Club to undertake another parade for the benefit of the Warrington Infirm- ary and Dispensary and the District Nursing Association. The parade &c, will take place on Saturday, the 23rd of August, and an effort is being made to make the spectacle even more interesting and suncessful than its predecessor. The parade will be followed by a smoking concert at which the prizes for the best caricatures and turnouts will be distributed. Warrington people generally take kindly to displays of this kind for charitable purposes and if the forth- coming function is favoured by fine weather there is every reason to believe that the two institutions mentioned will receive considerable financial help by the parade. Both charities require a large annual income for their maintenance and a sub- stantial subscription as the result of the parade will be heartily welcome.

ST. PAUL'S MOTHERS' MEETING fhe women's society connected witn the above church held their annual excursion on Monday, when the quaint old city of Chester was visited by about fifty ladies accompanied by the Rev Alfred Wells, superintendent minister, and Mrs Wells, president of the Society. The party were conveyed in two large brakes, an i after a most delightful run through the country, the city was reached in suffi- cient time to allow all the excursionists to inspect the interesting sights of the Cathedral city. The wane the rows, the Castle,the park,&c, &c,were each in turn visited. A portion also went up the River Dee as far as Eccleston Ferry from which place the Duke of Westminster's grounds were visited. Tea was subsequently' served at the Holborn restaurant. The home journey, which was a most enjoyable run, was made expeditiously, Runcorn being reached about 8 p.m. The secretaries in charge of the arrangements were the Nlisses Wells and N Harrison to whose exertions must be ascribed much of the pleasure of the trip. MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN. The annual summer excursion in connection with the Sunday school of the Seamen's Institute was held on Monday, when about 150 scholars and teachers left Runcorn for a day'souting at Grappen- hall. As is customary the party were conveyed by boat up the Bridgewater Canal starting from the Top Locks at 8.30 am. At Delph Bridge a halt was made for a short time, more excursionists joining the party. The destination London Bridge, was reached in good time the sail through the country being of a most enjoyable description. Happily the atmospheric conditions were all that could be de- sired, everything conspiring to make the excursion a thorough success. At Grappenball a large field had been requisitioned for the special benefit of the youngsters and all were speedily engaged in the various games which youth and ingenuity invent. Refreshments were provided on board the boat for the children. After a thoroughly enjoyable day had been spent, the return journey was made, Runcorn being reached about 8 pm. The entire arrangements were carried out by Mis- sioner Shaw, to whom must be ascribed not a little of the success of the trip.

RUNCORN FREE LIBRARY. Tne monthly meeting of the Library Committee was held in the Council Chamber on Tuesday, Mr Pope in the chair. The librarian's report for the past month showed that the library had been open 17 days and that the total number of issues was 1349 being a daily average of 79, an increase of 52 I • in the reference department and a decrease of 315 in the lending department compared with the corresponding month last year. The number of issues in each department was as follow! : —Theology, 16; philosophy, 3; science 50; history, 50; poetry 9 ; fiction, 1,000 ; miscel- laneous, 108 ; French literature, 2 ; [BUBB. 8; I After some discussion the report was adopted.— Accounts amounting to .£5 10s 3d were passed for payment. The receipts for the past month totalled .£1 103 63 including fines, Is 21; tickets,6s 6d; cata- logues, Is 81, Among several gifts of books were some valuable catalogues from the British Museum and a book on " The Education Crises" from Sir J T Brunner. It was decided to close the Library on the day of the King's Coronation the 9th August but to keep the reading room open. MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN. RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A C3RRESPONDENT.]

Up to the time of writing there has been no official pronouncement as to the manner in which Runcorn is likely to celebrate the King's Corona- tion. Mr Taylor is at present away from home..and of course nothing can be done until he returns. It is rumoured that the Friendly societies will hold a demonstration and that the ::unday sAiools will paradeathe town. But it is extremely unlikely that there will be an official parade.

This week has seen quite an exodus of pleasure seekers from Runcorn in search of amusement. All the Sunday schools with one or two exceptions, have taken advantage of the flue weather to hold their anntsal excursion. The majority have visited Mersey View—a resort which affords plenty of scope for juveniles bent on amu-ement.

"Lt is satisfactory to know that the idle rumours so persistently circulated a few days ago respecting the stability of the stone approaches of the Trans- porter:Bridge sre entirely without foundation. A semi-official denial will be lona(' in auotter column.

The moral atmosphere of Runcorn is getting purer. The past week has witnessed an almost en- tire cessation of police proceedings. Whether this is attributable to the moral nature of a certaiii sec- tion undergoing a process of evolution or whether the improvement be due to tne indefatigable exer- tions of the gentlemen in blue is difficult t 4 say.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTO NN.

PTi CORRESPONDENT].

The dealing out of water to consumers by the District Cni.ncil is of a.very erratic nature. Not many days ago one portion of the township was entirely without nature's liquid for the best part of two days, one of which was liundav, and many were the devices resorte t to in obtaining a sufficient quantity tp meet Cie wants of the household. It was peculiar to see the heads of households, armed with all manner of utensils, wending their way to a discovered source of supply, and many were the adverse criticisms hurled at the absent head of the Chairman of the Water Committee. Never since the arrangement wito the Railway Company was discontinued have we had a constant and regular supply of water, and if precedent is anything to go by, we never shall.

Probably the shortage of water has semething to do with the extremely offensive smells which assail us as we walk along the streets. From the man- holes in various parts of the district, smells suffici- ent to cause diphtheria and other kindred diseases arise,which betoken insufficisnt flushing of sewers. Such a state of things should not be allowed to exist, and it is high time the Surveyor had his serious attention drawn to it. Along the Nlesnes footpath which is a favourite sun greatly fre- quented walk, the same stare of things exists, but no effort has hitherto been made to remedy it. Pro- ably when an epidemic breaks out, some enquiry will be made, bu-, until then I suppose we must po,sess our souls in patience.

In spite of all opposition and against the criticisms of those in high places the floral bazaar in aid of the proposed cottage hospital was held last week,aad has been successful. The committee who carried it through have only the working people ot Earle3town and Newton,who loyally stood by them, to thank for their support. The trades- people have also given mot valuable asaistance,and they have earned the grateful thanks of the com- mittee. Those who first put the hospital scheme to the front have, to a certain extent stood aside, and done their best go wreck it, but being corn- mitted, the Bazaar Committee determined to see the bazaar through at all events, and their deter- mination has met with the reward it deserved. This Pis only another illustration of what a body of working men can do, when loyally supported by I their colleagues, in spite of the covert opposition of the classes. There is no doubt a cottage hospital will be provided and it is the determination of the heroic few to carry their efforts to a successful conclusion.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR REPRESEN- TATION. Conference of working Class Organ- izat:ons at Warrington.

IThe subject of labour representation was under I discussion at a conference of working cUiss organ- izations held in the Druids' Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday evening. The object of the conference, which was convened by the Trades and Labour Council, was to consider the formation of a Workers' Election Committee in Warrington. The aim of the proposed committee, as stared on the circular calling the meeting, 'mania be " to educate the workers to a greater desire for direct labour re- presentation, and to give eff,ct to that desire by promo t lug the candidature of working ciass repre- sentatives on all publicly-elected governing bodies who shall be independent of any political party." There was a representative attendance at the con- ferenc,e, Mr John Edwardson, President of the Trades and Labour Council, presiding. Mr F Slim (secretary of the Trades Council) explamed the circumstancs which led to the convening of the meeting. He pointed out that the labour representation movement bad been growing for a number of years; and it was mainly owing to the energy displayed by the Independent Labour Party that conferences similar to that one had been arranged in various towns. Three or four months ago a resolution was fpassed by the Trades' Coun- cil instructing the Executive Committee to arrange that conference- They found that in the, past the Trades Council had contested various local elections with, in the main, very poor results. At the last Board of Guardians election they nominate I Mr Plinston for Bewsay Ward, and the result of the contest, trom a labour standpoint, was an utter failure. He, along with otners, thought it was not fair that the Trades Council as a bady should undertake any contest wnen they could not guarantee that the workingmen of Warrington were going to work and vote for (heir candidate. The meeting was called to ascertain the feeling of the workingmen towards labsur repreientutan. It had been said that Warrington was a " good old Tory town." That might be so, but he believed that:the men of Warrington were apathetic, because they ha I not begun to consider the question. He hopea the Society would in future see fit to assist the movement like Co-operative Soci ities in other towns had done.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe P. S. A. Society. There was an average attendance at the meeting of this society en Sunday afternoon. Mr Healey presided and spoke upon " Cheerfulness." The speaker for the occasion was Mr Moss, of Stockton Eleath,who delivered a thoughtful address on " Character." A quartet paity censistiog of Miss Bridge, Miss Woodland,Mr Corrin and Mr Spruce very effieienly centributed two quartettes entitled " Ged so loved the world "arid " God is a sprit." Mr W H Pay ten, Moe. Bac., presided at the organ. Tee service was also taken part in by Mr Downes, Mr David Owen and Mr B 'arrar. The lad- named gentlemen gentlemen read a letter from the Rev John Yenge, who is recruiting his health at Elarrogate. Baptist Chapel, Golborne-street. Toe Pastor (Rev El Dunnieo). delivered the first of a series if sermons on the " Verities of the Christian Faith," in the above church on Sunday morning. There was a large congregation, in the course of his sermon he said he did not euppese they would agree with all he would say, but that was only what was to be expeeed. and his obj-ct would be accomplished if only he might inspire the young men tee take an intelligent interest in the subjects disousafd. No careful observer of the times could fail to no.ice that they were in a state of transition in the religious world ; at any rate they had come to the parting of the ways. Many of the elJer systems if belief had gone and many others were going, but, for his port he saw no cause for •nxiety or panic. Some people mistook these beliefe and dogmas for re- ligion itself, and were trunbline lest the foundations of the Cairch were being destroyed, but that was not so. Their little systems had their day and ceased to be, but the vital esseutial lite of the Church remained ever the ' same. rue pastor then spoke on the subject of Bible difficulties and noted that the evidences for Christianity rested upon the Resurrection, the testimony of the Gospels and Epistles and on the history of the Church and not upon the strange doings of an ass or a whale. Whether they were able to accept the literal acouracy of these stories or net, it was extremely illogical to re- fuse to accept the lesson they conveyed, and extremely foolish to refuse to believe in the Bible at all. The preacher spoke ter same time upon the various difficul- ties ; and, in conclusion, get e the testimony of our greatest men respecting the Bible. Re appealed to them all to read the book in an earnest, reverent, manner, without any preconceived ideas, and if they did that they need not fear the result. Buckley-street Methodist Free Church.

The choir sermons were preached in this church on Sunday, by Mr Andrew Scriven. In the morning the Preacher drew special attention to the musical portion of the church's service, urging the choir to do their utmost to make the services bright and influeatial by renderiag their tongs of praise in all sincerity. ,If the singing was to b 3 eefactive, they should study to be in earnest in the rendition of each piece If music. Miss Fairhurst sang" Toou knowest, Lord, Thee knowest all about me." A musical service was held in the after- noon, presided over by Mr E Plinston. Mr Morton delivered an address upon " Miriam's song." Mr W Looker effeetively rendered the solos " There is a green hill far away," and" Man of Sorrows." Mrs Moores , sweet voioe was heard to advantage le "Sweetly sad vision " and " Our owe dear home" Mr Woods was much appreciated ire " The star of Bethlehem " and a recitative air from " Elijah." The choir rendered the anthem " Abide with me." Mr Albert E Trow was the accompanist, and Mr Ryder con lucted the choir. The evening, service was well attended, when the pulpit wee again occupied by Mr SCriVerl, who took for his text " As Moses lilted up the serpent in the wilderness, even so must the Son of Man be lifted up, that whosoever believeth on Him should not perish, but hays everlasting life." Mr Woods sang " Sun of my soul," and special hymns and anthems were rendered by the choir. The Bishop of Liverpool at St. Paul's Church. Special services were held in this Church en Sueday, the offertories being in aid of the:Church Rnovation Fund. The preacher at the morning service was the Rev J E Wainwright. M.A., (Battersea Training College). In the afternoon the Bishop of Liverpool delivered an address to men only, the service being largely attended. His Lordship also preached in the evening, when every seat in the Church was occupied. The Bishop took for his text the words " And the house, when it was in building, was built of stone made ready before it was brought thither : so that there was ceither hammer nor axe, nor any tool of iron heard in the house while it was in building."—l. Kings vi. 7. The Bishop made an earoest appeal to the congregation to give liberally towards the renocatioe fund of the church. tte stated that he could not help being s ruck when he arrived at the church that afternoon, and again that evening, at the exceeding neatness and beauty of the elsurchyard. It was kept as God's Acre ought to be kept, namely, with care, love, and reverence. The work redounded to the credit of all who had been responsible for it. The same svirit of reverence which had prompted them to keep God's Acre se beautiful prompted them also to keep God's house equally well. In a few years' time that house, so we'l kept at present, would need again the brush of the painter and trae care of the carpenter. The cost would be great for the building was urge, and to pay the whole amount in a single year would be a heavy draw en their finances as a ceurcn and congregation. Therefore, like prudent men, they were preparing for the improvement when it belame nec,ssary to make it. During the service special hymns were sung, and the anthem " Glory to• Thee, my God, this night," was creditably rendered by the choir.

PEOPLE ARE EATING LESS.

At the annual meeting of a well-known Refreshment House Co. the other day, it was officially reponed that the numner of people served had no: decreased, but the quantity of food consumed had. This means so much °inlet loss of strength ; for as soon as the food supply is diminished the bodily strength declines. Chas. Forde's Bile Beans will keep an edge on your appetite during summer's trying weather, and will prevent you from being "run do on "or becoming a sufferer from in- digestioo's evil. Mrs Mary A Dodsworth of Shipyard Row, Selby, says :—" I could take very little food be- cause of indigestion. Meat was to me uneatable. E2gs caused me pain- Bread, tea, bacon and ordinary articles of diet made me so sick that I hated to touch food at all. The result was I became so weak and jaded that I was hardly able to walk. To-day I am in robust health ; and the difference in me is due solely to Bile Beans. I am a living example of what vast changes they can work." Every man and woman who has no appetite, should remembsr Mrs Dodsworlb. Bile Beans are sold by all chemistf at one and three-half-pence or two and nine. STOCKTON HEATH.

GARDEN FETE AT HILL CLIFFE. —A garden fete organized by Mrs•llenry Greenall, with the object of aiding the scholarship fund for St Elpsdn's Clergy Daughters' School (in which institution she manifest much • interest) was held on Monday afternoon in her picturesque grounds at Hilt Cliffs, and was attended with gratifying success. The attendance nuilhbered over 500.Bts'itlee those taking an active part in:the event there were among those present the Mayoress of Warrington (Mrs J C Parr), Mr Roger Parr, Mr W P Rylands, NI J E Seeker, the Rev Canon Willis and 'Mrs Willis, Rev A S Cameron, Rev G Diekinsen, Mrs Eokersley (Southport) , Mrs F A Frost, Mrs Vere Davies, Mr Leonard Davies, Mr W Richardson, Miss Kennedy, the Misses Litton and Mrs Fletcher (Whitley). There were a variety of attrac- tions to amuse. The most fun was to be had round the see-saw,on which numerous pillow fights took place. This was under the direction of Mr T J Lee and Mr J Birtles. Several swing boats were superintended by Mr F G Taylor Mr. Bradshaw had charged of the " Aunt Sallies," and Mr H J Westbrook, of the cocoanuts; Mr W H J Jenkins managed a lucky table; Mr B Newcombe and Mr J Gray a fish pond ; Mr G H Smith, a croquet target ; and Mr A M Ker. ping-pong tables. Two large mar- quees were rected, in one of which refreshments were served, while the other contained a fancy work atall, which was presided over by pupi's from the Clergy Daughtere' School, who contributed the articles for sale, together with a fruit and flower stall, over which Mrs Henry Greenall presided and was asiisted by Mrs Wright Mrs Myles Davies, Mrs Glazsbrook, Mrs Greenall, and Miss Massey. The depot band from the Barracka was in attendance and played for dancing in the evening,the arrangements for which were in the hands of Mr New- combe. A croquet tournament held on Saturday and Monday was watched with considerable interest. There were 28 entries. Mr Mclntyre and Mr E J Hall had charged of the arrraneemente at the Cate. The proceed- ings fittingly closed with a firework display.

Local Volunteers Returning Home.

The Mayor has received an intimation from Colonel Firzherbert that two officers and 42 rank and file of the Volunteer Service Company of the Liverpool Regiment ,tre due at Southamptm on F.S. " German on let August and also that Lent F Willcocks and 26 men of the Warrington 'Volunteers are due at Southampton on the same date.

Junior Conservatives at Chester and Farndon. The annual picnic in connection with the War- rington Junior Conservative Association took place on Saturday, Chester and Farndon being visited Ihe party numbering 105, of whom 93 were mem- bers of the Association, left Bank Quay Station at 12 30 and reached Chester in about an hour. They at once proceeded to the Groves where the steamer " Ontario "was in waiting to take them to Fara- don. The weather conditions were not very favour- able, but the sail was much enjoyed. On returning to Chester the party sat down to tea at the City Café. After the meal, Mr W L Williams proposed that a telegram be sent to Mr Balfour in the following terms :—" That the members of the Warrington Junior Conservative Association warmly congratulates the Right Hon. A J Balfour, M P., on the well-earned distinction conferred upon him by his elevation to the position of Prime Minister, and upon the universal approval with which his selection has been received." He apologized for the absence that day of their Presi- dent, Mr Roger Parr, Mr E Draper, Mr J H Smet- hurst. and Councillor A Bennett —A vote of thanks was accorded to the following committee, who con- ducted the arrangements for the party :—Messrs W L Williams (chairman), S Thorley (vice-chair- man), H Hayes (secretary),W C Brookes (treasurer), J Strettle,H B Houghton,J Seed, and J J Boardman' The party afterwards had a walk round the ancient city, and commenced the return journey at ten o'clock, having had an enjoyable outing under the circumstances.

Warrington Conservatives and the New Premier.

A meeting of the Council of the Conservative Association was held on Monday evening in the Assembly Room of. the Conservative Club, under the presidency of Mr John White. There was a good attendance of members.—On the motion of Coun- cillor Smethurst, secanded by Air George Jolley. Mr White was re-elected chairman. Alderman Fran- comb was re elected vice-chairman.—The following resolution was moved by the Chairman,seconded by Mr Ed Draper, supported by Mr W L carried unanimously :—" That this meeting of the Council of the Warrington Conservative Association desires to place on record its high appreciation of the invaluable services rendered to the Empire by the Marquis of Salisbury for more than a quarter of a century, and, whilst congratulating. Mr Arthur J Balfour upon his appointment as Prime Minister, assures him that he and the Government of which

he is the head may depend upon the continued sup- port of the Unionist party in Warrington."—A vote of thanks having been accorded to Mr White for presiding," God save the King" was sang, and the proceedings terminated.

A man was arrested /ale on Tuesday night in con- nection with the excessive robbery from the Belfast Free Library of costly articles lent by the South Kensington Museum.

THE CORONATION.

How the Warrington Go-operative Society Will Commemorate the Event.

Handsome Donation to the Infirmary.

Mr T Birch%ll presided on Monday evening at the Co-operative Hall, Saakey-street, over the 166th quar- terly meeting of the Waarington Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society, Limited. In the course of his speech, in moving the adoption of the report and toe balance sheet, he said they had arrived at a very event- ful period in the history of the country. They had seen asce Wing the Throw+ the son of the noblest monarch that bad ever ruled over the destinies of this country. The King was loved and trusted by his peop'e, and he believed be ty.arld prove a worthy son of his mother. The Society were desirous of marking their satisfaction and loyalty by something that ahauld not be merely of an ephemeral character: A great deal of money had been spent on things that were merely fizzles, such as fireworks, which afforded a tem- porary gratification and ultra soon all over, but they in that society wished to mark their sense of the approaching Coronation by supporting one of the noblest inetitutions in the borough—they meant the Infirmary—by giving a donation of £5OO from the Eduestion Reserve Fund. (Applause). That sum would be fir abe at the Infirmary to be called after the name of the society. They all knew the deep interest hia Majesty took in institutions for the railer of suffering, and would remember that when someone askad what was the best means of presentmg a gift to him be said "Support the hospitals." That was a very noble sentiment. (Rear, hear and applause.) The minutes of a previous meeting were then read, in which it was suzgesr,ed that the sum of £5OO should be given to the Infirmary, the bed to be called "The Warrington Co-operative Society Coronation Endow- ment Bad." . .

_ Mr J CROWTHER approved of the suggestion, but thought the Nursing Association was aleo descrOing of support. Tne CHAIRMAN thought the suggestion jost made was a good one and it would be taken into consideration by the committee. Mr Monks, the founder ot the War- rington and District Nursing A'sociation, and who at considerable cost helped to keep it going, was the first secretary of the Co-oporative Society. Be could assure the meeting that she Nursing Association had a warm place in all their hearts. The minutes were then agreed to, and the report adopted.

Warrington Sunda/ School Teachers' Association.

The quarterly meeting of this Association was held at Padgate on Saturday afternoon. The Vicar- age garden was open to visitors at three o'clock,and tea was afterwards provided in the schools. A record number (130) sat down. In the evening a service was held in the church, when an address was delivered by the Rev, A A. Markham, vicar of St. Jude's, Liverpool. The gathering was a very successful one.

EVENTS OF THE NffnEK

ILLUSTRATED.

It seems almost incredible that within another fortnight the postponed Coronation will be un fait accompli; but such is indeed the case. For some time it has been an open secret that his Majesty wished the delay to be as short as possible, as his convalescence has been so marvellously rapid; and first it was tentatively announced that it would take place between the Bth and the 12th of August. At the end of last week the Earl Marshal made it known that Saturday, August 9th, was the chosen day—the day which had immediately been decided upon by the public mind as the most convenient of those mentioned. Already the nation have had two whole holidays in celebration of the event, and it was felt on every side that the choice of a Saturday would dislocate things less than any other day, as it was already a half-holiday. Of course, the Coronation will not carry nearly so much interest as it would have done in June, as much in connection with it has been discounted by partial celebrations; and naturally for the sake of his Majesty's health everything will be as far as possible curtailed. THE CORONATION:

REDUCED FACSIMILE OF THE OFFICIAL ANNOUNCEMENT OF THE DATE IN THE GAZETTE.

instance will be cut down to the utmost point, lest the; strain should prove too great for the King, while the procession will only pass along , the shorter route—from Buckingham Palace to Westminster, vid the Mall and Whitehall, and back, 'yid St. James's Street, Piccadilly, and Constitution Hill. There will, also, be no second procession, as had been previously arranged, along the Diamond Jubilee route, through the Strand, and South London, but the King hopes to carry out this part of the programme in October, and has written to the Mayor of Southwark to this effect. At present his physicians do not consider him fit for such a trying ordeal, and the main object of everyone concerned is to get the Corona- tion safely over without any relapse in his Majesty's health. This will disappoint the vast majority of those who were to have seen the great pageant, but under the circumstances they bow to the decision without, a murmur, and will gladly wait till October when the procession will celebrate the Kines restoration to health as much as the Coronation. Amongst other things there will be no foreign deputations from the other Powers for many obilous reasons, each country being represented at the ceremony by its Ambassador at the Court of St. James's, and, though this will largely curtail the display in the Abbey and the interest of the procession, it will relieve the King of many great social responsibilities which would be entailed by their entertainment. One point which has caused general delight is the decision not to abandon the Naval Review, which will be held during the week after the ceremony. The whole fleet is to assemble at Spithead after the manceuvring is over, to be in readiness for the event, and there it will be joined, as it was in June, by representative ships from all the foreign fleets. People in general are delighted at the promptness which has been shown in all quarters in the reorganisation of the Coronation programme, which, though necessarily shortened, will un- doubtedly be full of interest and worthy of the historic occasion. The shortness a the route will probably create a boom in seats, and there is a great rush to secure places in the boats chartered for the Naval Review. His Majesty is certain of a rousing reception from all assembled, as his illness has made his popularity greater than ever, and the nation is anxious to show that it fully appreciates she pluck he has CAPTAIN LANIBTON,

THE KING'S YACHT AND HER CAPTAIN.

exhibited from first to last, not only during the actual operation itself, but in his vain attempt to carry things through as arranged, at the risk of his very life. Meanwhile, the King is progressing as favourably as possible on board the Royal Yacht, and gaining strength day by day. In fact, from the very day of the operation he has made the most consistent progress, thanks to the unremitting care of his physicians, and never for an hour has he shown signs even of delay in his recov.ery. It is due to this pleasing fact that it hbo been possible to reorganise things for the Coronation ceremony so quickly—far more quickly than was at first anticipated. Since his Majesty reached Cowes, having taken the journey splendidly, his convalescence has been especially rapid, and it is expected that by the 7th or Bth of August, when he will return to town, he will be in better health than he has been in for years. Report says that he is looking particularly well, and considerably thinner than before his breakdown.

Nobody was surprised at the resignation of Lord Cadogan when it fell to Mr. Balfour's lot to reorganise the Unionist Cabinet, as it has been an open secret since last October that he was anxious to retire from the post of Lord Lieutenant of Ireland. He did not wish to re- join the Cabinet at all after the Gen deraild oElesctiot% but oblige Lord Sails- bury, and latterly remained in office in view of the King's proposed visit to Ireland, which has not yet taken place Owing to various circumstances. So, naturally, when a new Premier took office and a re- distribution of places in the Cabinet became necessary, hi s portfolio was the first to go in. Earl Cadogan has LORD OADOGAN held this peon- THE RETIRING LORD•LIEUTENANT liarrly trying and OF IRELAND. difficult office _ _

since 1895, when he succeeded the Earl of Crewe, upon the downfall of Lord Rose- bery's short term of Office, and amongst the Irish people themselves seldom has there been a more popular Lord Lieutenant. In fact, for an English.' man he went down wonderfully well with the whole of the Irish nation, and his term of office was socially one of the Most brilliant ever seen. Politically, however, he laid .himself open to great criticism, especially amongst his friends. and it is whispered in Government circles that he has at times considerably embarrassed his Unionist .eoarfreres. Suffice to say that relations between 'Dublin Castle and the Ileading Unionists of,

Being a member of the Cabinet, he WO primarily responsible for the Irish policy of he Administration as Mr. George Wyndham, the Chie,, Secretary, had no seat in the supreme politio-OJ+ council. However, the promotion of the latter. i.s! now assured; and the new Lord Lieutenant vril-` not be in the ring of the Cabinet and, therefore,. more a social leader than a political one. Of Lorct Cadogan it may safely be said that he has acted! very conscientiously and has played the temporal, role of local King with lavish brilliance and grese expenditure from his private purse. It was expected that he would have figured in the Coronado!! Honours' list, but now it appears that his revrar was probably held over in view of his retirement. It is announced that the new Lord Mayor or London is to be Sir Marcus Samuel, who ht been an Alderman since 1891, and was Shaw in 1894-5. He was knighted in 1898 in connee- tion with the services he rendered to Victorious, and has all through his career beeo a very successful man. . In his young days 115 travelled widely in the East and Japan, where he established a business, and it was he who first introduced the transport of petroleum i n bulk through the Suez Canal. The Lord Mayor- Elect, who is only forty - nine years of age, lives in Portland Place, and has a country house in Kent. I FANCY DRESS .PARADE AND

CARNIVAL. To be held at Warrington in August. --____

It has now been definitely decided to hold another fancy dress cycle parade and carnival at Warrington on Angus-. 23rd in aid of the funds of the Infirmary and Dis- trict Nursing Association. The matter was under con- sideration at a meeting held at the Patten Arms Hotel on 'Thursday evening, !last week and convened by the Secretary of the Warrington Cycling Club (Mr E Picka- vance,jun),Therewas an encouraging attendance,amongst those present in addition to members of the Cycling Club, being Councillor John Roberts and Mr Joseph Taylor (representing the Infirmary Workingmen's Committee). Mr " Pa " Davies was voted to the chair, and in the course of his remarks, said ho had no doubt that when occasion arose, the members of the Cycling Club would doltheir duty as they did in 1900. It was then unanimously decided to hold a parade and carnival in aid of the Infirmary and District Nursing Association.

The CHAIRMAN said they were entering upon an ardu- ous duty, but one to which they would all willingly put their hands. He hoped they would all do their utmost for the success of the parade,and if they did so there Was no reason why they should not double the amount realizA in 1900. They learnt some lessons in 1900 which would be useful to them this year. He desired it to be known that they welcomed the assistance of everyone—what- ever branch of athletics cr society they belonged to. With regard to cyclists, he thought they would by their action in this matter be doing something to show the reason of their existence. Time was, when cyclists and athletes in general wore looked upon with some show of contempt, but he thought they had justified their exist- ence on more occasions than one by the efforts they had made in the interests of charity. All they asked for in connection with the fortt,z,mling parade Was the assist- ance of the public—of those whom the Infirmary was intended to becefit particularly. Athletes themselves rarely required the institution because they were very rarely ill, and no one could say that they were " boom- ing " the parade for their own benefil% They were throwing themselves heart and soul into the work, and he hoped that as a result of the parade they would be able to hand over a very handsome sum to the Infirmary and Nursing Association. PRESIDENT AND VICE-PRESIDENT.

Mr. PICKAYANCE informed the meeting that he wrote to the Mayor asking him to act as President of the parade, and to Mr. F. Monks, J.P , asking him to accept the office of Vice-president. The Town Clerk replied on behalf of the Mayor, intimating his Worship's willingness to become Pres'd.,nt. Mr. Monks wrote con- senting to act as Vice-president and expressing the hope that great success would reward the labours of those who were nromoting the parade. The meetineformally elected the Mayor President, and Mr P. Moults, Vice-president.

OTHER OFFICIALS. Mr D. DAViPS was elected chairman of the General Committee; Councillor John Roberts and Mr W H Bellian, vice-Chairmen ; and Mr E Pickavance, secretary. In returning thanks for his election, Mr PICEAVANCE said they could rest assurred that he would do his very best for the success of the parade and he was certain that he would be well backed-up by all the members of the Club. Already he had received more offers of help than he had received up to the night before the parade in 1900. Scores of ladies and gentlemen had offered their services, and he thought they would have a very good parade. Mr CHARLES LEIGH was then elected treasurer, and Mr F J Hogan, reporting hon secretary. The following secretaries were elected In attend to sections of the parade :—Harriers' section, Mr T Whit- ham ; footballers' s-ction, Mr T P Bradshaw ; section, Messrs H II Evans and J C Robinson,

CO3IMITTEr ,„

The following committees were elected with power to add to their number : FINANCH COMMITTEE :—Mesqrs Jolrnnh Taylor. IV Taylor, C Leigh, W Brown, C Wells, and C El Woods (sectretarv)• PRIZE COMMITTEE :—CMIDCiIIOr J %Vflkinson. MeSl Joseph Taylor, Isaac Wilkinson, J L Davenport, W El Bellian, and W Habloprley (seoretary). MARIYALS COMMITTEE :—MeSSI'e II Ashton (chief- marshal). WARRINGTON WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. Picnic to Bollington.

Beautifully fine weather favoured the annual picnic in connection with the Warrington Women's Liberal Association which took place on ThursdGy afternoon last week,Bollington in Cheshire was again chosen as the destination, and over fifty persons participated in the outing. Two large conveyances were supplied by Mr Hugh F Greenough for the accommodation of the party, and a start was made from the Liberal Club shortly after twelve o'clock. The party was mainly composed of ladies, the male sax being in a hopeless minority. Mr Councillor Tinuion, Mr Hill, Mr Turner, Mr Booth and two Press representatives made up the male element. The ladies present included Mrs Armitage (president), Mrs li Roberts (treasurer), Mrs C Jackson Holmes (secretary), Mrs R Garnett, Mrs Clay, Mrs Walker, Mrs Furness, and Mrs Tinnion. Mrs Armitage joined the party at Bollington, she having journeyed there in her private brougham. On leaving smoky Warrington behind, the party appreciated to the full, the pure air of the country. Bathed in the bright sunshine, the country licked at its best. The crops were well advanced and as they waited in the gentle breeze, every shade of green was disclosed. In many fields, the merry noise of the reaping machine was to be heard, and the air was filled with the fragrance of new mown hay. The route taken was via Grappenhall and High Legh. On reaching High Legh schools, the driver turned to the lett and shortened the journey to Bollington. it was intended to take a wider sweep of country by journeying via Rostherne, but the driver willed otherwise, and it was decided to visit Rostherne on the return journey. Bollington was reached about two o'clock and on leaving the conveyance, the party paid a visit to Dunham Park,the residence of the Earl of Stamford and Warrington. The stroll through the spacious d miesne, in which numerous young deer, pheasants and rabbits were to be seen, was greatly enjoyed. At 4.30, the party gathered at the Swan Hotel, where a capital knife and fork tea was provided. After tea, Mrs Armitage, the President of the Association,in the course of a few observations,said she was very glad to see so many present. She intimated that the annual tea party would be held at the beginning of Octmber at the commencement of the winter season and she would like to see as many there as they had ',hat afternoon. It was proposed to have some lectures and meetings during the winter and she would be glad if they would endeavour to rally round their gcod old cause. (Hear y arjobueranre.) The . commenced at 6.30. A short stay was made at Rostherne where the party had an opportunity of walking through the church- yard and feasting their eyes en the pretty scenery of the Mere. Warrington was safely reached at 9.30 all having spelV, an enjoyable afternoon.

CLERGY INSTITUTIONS.

Anniversary at Warrington.

The anniversary of the Clergy Widows" and Orphans' Society and the Clergy Daughters' School, two charitable institutions common to the dioceses of Chester, Manchester, and Liverpool, was observed at Warrington otr Wednesday. Mr H H Hornby, of Liverpool, the president for the year, presided at 'a meeting of subscribers held in the Training College. Amongst others present were the Bishop of Liverpool (Or Chavasse), the Dean of Manchester (Dr Maclure), Archdeacon Fletcher, Canon Gore,the Rev Canon Penrhyn (Icon secretary of the Clergy Widows' and Orphans' Pensions Fund), the Rev Canon Willis (hon secretary of the Clergy Daughters' School,. the Revs S A K Sylvester,James Carson, E C E Carleton, and H Harry; Dr Watkins, and Messrs W P Fullaghar, Joynson, and Grof ton. The Bishop of Chester wrote, explaining that a long-stanCing engagement to be away from Chester at a remote distance during the latter half of July prevented him from attending. THE REPORTS.

Rev Canon PENRHYN,hOII secretary of the Clergy Widows and Orphans Relief fund submitted his annual report, in which he pointed out that there was a pleasing variation this year as they had not to tell of any vacancy by death in their number. The only alteration in the li.ts was the substitution of the Bishop of Burnley for that of Canon Hoskyns to whom they would express their best wishes on his promotion to the episcopal office. No other names having been submitted to the secretarias,the following names were submitted for confirmation : Chester, Rev Canon H Blackburne, Rev Canon Turner, Lord Egerton of Tatton and Mr T 1.1 Lyon; Manchester, Rev Canon Stowell, Rev J A Rushton and Messrs W S Kinde and J Scantling; Liverpool, Rev Canon Spooner, Rev R G Matthews:: Mr It D'A Willis and Captain Withington. Of those to whom grants were made last year two widows and one orphan from the Manchester diocese had since died and one widow from Chester had withdrawn her application. On the other hand there were two new widows' applications from Manchester, and one each from Chester and Liverpool, with one orphan from Chester. The meeting was asked to sanction pensions and donations to the amount of £1,770, and if the recommendations of the committee were approved there would be on the list of grantees from Chester, 17 widows and 16 orphans; from Manchester,3l widows and 17 orphans; from Liver- pool, 15 widows and 10 orphans. The income for the year ended December 1901 was £2OlO 163 10d. This income was by no means satisfactory ;as al- though the total was slightly in excess of last year (..E2003 123 4cl), both subscriptions and collections were lower and the adverse balance of £304 16s Bd, bad risen to £448 4s while there was no appearance of diminution in the number of applicants for grants,or in the urgency of their need. Under these circumstances, the committee felt that they were fully justified in appealing to the three dioceses to increase the support they had hitherto given to this most excellent institution. Tli& CLERGY DAUGHTERS' SCHOOL

The Rev. CANON WILLIS (bon. sec., of the Clergy Daughters' School) reported that the committee of management, bad the satisfaction of feeling that the results of the examinations received since the last anniversary could hardly be better. In tne Cambridge Higher Local of 9 pupils who took various groups, G were completel3, successful in what they attempted, and 2 partially so, Of all the candidates in England who took religious know- ledge 7 only were placed in the first class, and 2 of these 7 whose names deserve mention—Annie P Jones and Ellen Morgan—were pupils of St Elphins. Three only obtained the mark of distinction, and of these 3, Annie Jones was one. Some of the results of the scripture in the Oxford local examina- tion were also especially satisfactory. In the pre- liminary examination the only two girls who were eligible for distinction in this subject both won viz., Grace Blakelock and Gwendoline Birch Jones. Grace Blakelock was the:first candidate in England in this subject and Gwendoline Birch Jones equal sixth. 1 hree girls out of four passed the senior Latin examination, five out of six passed the senior French, one with distinction and the only girl who took the senior German passed. The whole of the school who took the Oxford local examination were successful in arith- metic and three out of the five in mathematics. With regard to the graft, the headmistress reported that in the place of Miss Ridley whose departure will be much regretted by all, she had appointed a mathe- matical mistress, Mies WriAbt. who won a mathe- matical scholarship to Girton, and who had this year taken a second class in the mathematical tripes at Cambridge: She had also, in accordance with the sanction of the committee, appointed a science mistress, Miss Tymis who is a Newnham student, and has just taken a first class in the natural science tripos. The health of the girls throughout the year had been very good except in the case of two girls who suffered from a very severe attack of influenza during the summer term. Both were seriously ill for some time and had to be ,1 removed to their own homes. The conduct gener- ally had been most satisfactory. The sleeping ac- commodation would be considerably increased during the summer vacation, another dormitory over the large schoolroom hitherto occupied by the Training College, having been resumed. This would make adequate provision for the large number of pupils, 73 in all, who would be in attendance next term. In concluding their report the committee desired to express again their most grateful thanks to bliss Kennedy for the able management of the - school, and their extreme satisfaction at its sound financial position. At the end of December, 1900 there was a balance in hand after several years of debt, of £l5O. At the close of 1901 this balance bad increased to .£475 and at the present time it is over £6OO. Miss KENNEDY presented a lengthy and satisfactory report of the lower school for the year ending Mid- summer 1902, and the Rev F G Brabant, M.A. (C.C.C. Oxford) • report on toe work of St Elphin's girls' school.

===== 0002601_19020725_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PENSION FUND DEFICIT.

The PRESIDENT, in moving the adoption Of the report ' remarked that the first one was not satisfactory. IV was sad to think that the pension fund was so low. He could not imagine anything that appealed to the feelings of generous persons more than the relief of widows and orphans of the ciergy. It wa6 sad to think also that when a husband died his family had often to leave what had been their bome,to which they tad been very much attached for a long time, and a still greater anxiety was the want of means. Surely the laymen of the three dioceses would not allow thr.t state of things to remain longer, and a special appeal of the committee should 'receive admirable supoorr. The second report was very satisfactory, and Canon Penrhyn must have felt jealous when he heal(' Canon Willis say that he had £6OO in band. (Laughter.) Both the institutions deserved the support of the laymen of the three dioceses. (Applause.) The Rev Canon GoRE seconded, and said formerly it was a rule that they should not exceed the amount at their disposal, but now it seemed the demands upon them were so many that :he committee had felt them- selves justified in going somewhat into debt. Nothing could be said too much in praise of the work of the Clergy Daughters' School. The very facts that they had one girl who was first in all England and another sixth were in themselves sufficient testimony to the valuable work of the school. (Appliuse.) He thought the time had arrived when a better school should be provided for the daughters of the clergy, and tbat it should be in a better locality, and be a far more modern structure with better accommodation. A few years ago they were £BOO in debt.now they had £6OO in hand, and if they went on like that they would soon have money enough to build a handsome new school. He echoed what had been said about Miss Kennedy,who was a most efficient headmistress. Mr W P FIILLAGFIAR made an earnest appeal for sup- port on behalf of the pension fund. The stories that were told to them year atter year were most lamentable and pitiable ; acme of the incomes could hardly be called incomes, and yet the ages of some of the ladies ranged from 70 to ao. He hoped the Queen .Victoria fund and ether funds would be the means of raising the incomes of the clergy to a proper and satisfactory amount. They should at least be sufficient to live upon and not a starvation amount. (Hear, hear.) The Rev J CARSON said that year after year they had a lamentation in the report. It was the old story, but what -was going to be done to bring theappeal before thepublic? In his own neighbourhood nothing was being done and there was no increase of the subscriptions and no can- vassing. The matter ought to be brought before the three dioceses, although ho thought the case of Manchester was hopeless. (Laughter.) The diocese should be well canvassed. The DEAN Of MANCTIESTER said no one more deeply deplored than he did the great want of interest which was displayfd in the institutions, but with regard to Manchester a movement was being made to raise sub- scriptisms. Ho was afraid that the fact that the institu- tions were called the Warrington institutions in sermons and addresses had something to answer for their present financial position. The institutions belonged to three dioceses and not to Warrington alone. For some reason the claims of the institutions were not pushed as they -ought CO be, and be very much regretted that that was

so. Be would use We inliuence to bring the matter under the notice of the Diocese of Manchester. The Rev Canon WILLIS said the meeting must not count too much on the £6OO as a cmsiderahle Sum was needed for repairs. The reports were adoptel.

SERVICE IN FEE PARISH CHURCH. THE BISHOP OF LIVERPOOL AND CLERICAL INCOMES.

A short service was subsequently held in the Parish I Church, when the sermon was preached by the B:shop of Liverpiol. His Lordship said that if the Church allowed her clergy to marry, she must IP prepared to support a married clergy. She must give them a living wage sufficient to maintain not only themselves but their families- She must provide for the widows and orphans whom they left behind. A certain number of their clergy posseseed private means and were able to make provision for the future, hut that number was at present tending to decrasse. Clerical incomes were largely diminishing. During the two years he had worked in the diocese of Liverpool, he had been greatly impressed by the constant discovery of clergy who had very limited means of their own, who bad spent and were still spending not merely their private income,but much of their patronage upon churches, parishes, and schools; and these devoted men were not only not receiving an adequate stipend, but had actually impoverished themselves and children. They did not parade their generositv,they seldom spoke of it, they never complained. They accepted it as part of their duty. They regarded themselves as shepherds in charge of the flock of God,and they would not allow the interests ef G ed's flock to suffer,The Church of Eng- land must face to-day thi.3 serious problem of clerical poverty. It could be lersened by endowments on their poorest livings, but the process wets very costly and very stow. It could be alleviated in a small degree by the growth of the ancient and practical custom of Easter offerings, by funds such as the Clerical Susteotation Fund, and by encouraging the clergy to avail them- selves of every method of helping themselves that lay within their reach. It might be diminished in some , measure by bringing public opinion to hoar upon the question of early and improvident marriages among the clergy. But though such measures as these might in some degree mitigate the financial straits of the clergy, there must still remain a large amount of distress, and therefore the Widows' and Orphans' Society for which he pleaded that morning, was of incalculable value. It was to the laity as well as to the richer clergy, that they muat look for its support. An appeal for the widows and orphans of the men of Gad who were con- tent to forego the riches of this world and to do the work of God's ministers on a mere pittance, and who faithfully and gladly gave of their best in the service of their fellows, could not surely be made in vain. SPEECH BY THE MAYOR.

Luncheon was provided in the Clergy Daughters' School. _ _ _ _ _ .

The PRESIDENT (Mr. H. H. Elornby) proposed the toast of " Church and King." After alluding to the illness and recovery of the King, Mr Hornby said the Church also in past years had suffered keenly, but like the King, fortunately it had a strong constitution—(lauchter)— and had recovered in an Equally good manner as the King had &me. The toast of " Queen Alexandra, the Prince and Princess of Wales and the members of the Royal Family was afterwards drunk. Mr JOYNSON proposed " The Bishops and Clergy of the three dioceses."

The BISHOP OF LIVERPOOL, in reply to the toast,said he was certain that there were no clergy who were doing their work more earnestly and with greater self-sacrifice than the clergy of Lancashire and Cheshire. He went into the slums of Liverpool, and he found men living there, never parading their work, never boasting what they were doing, and living lives of true heroes, if heroism meant going out of self. Those men were doing I the noblest work, and were helping to elevate the minds' of the people, who would ether wise be discontented and I unruly, and have no education in tho great principles of Christianity. Be did not mean to say that the Church I was perfect, but she was manfully trying to cast eft the I slumber and neglect of past ages, and becoming what I she ought to be—not merely the Church of England, hut I the Church of tbe English people. (Applause). This could not be done until the laity worked with them. If the clergy and laity would work together for the pros- verity of the Church then nothing could stand against them. (Applause). The toast of "success and prosperity to the Warring- ton lostitutions " was proposed by Mr. FULLACILIER, and responded CO by the DEAN of MANCHESTER. • The Rev CANON GORE prcposed "The Town and Trade of Warrinetrn and said for more than 200 years the town had sh-iterf d the Warrirgt a inst;tutions If atlanY suture time the Clergy Daughters' echool was removed the Training College would be extended and would occupy the premises. The MAYOR of Warrington (Mr J. C. Parr) in respond- ing to the toast said be believed he might refer with some amount of satisfaction to what he believed was the very flourishing condition of Warrington to-day and he did this with all the greater freedom because be was only a young recruit to the municipal body and the embellishments and scientific improvements which had been made in Warringten were due to his predecessors in office and be could not take any credit for them. He considered that what had been done of late had transformed Warrington from a backward and some- what dark condition to that of a town of light and lead- ing of the present day. When they looked around at their widened streets and improved buildings (both public and private) ; at their tramways which were about to go north, south, east, and west; at their electric light ancl electric motive power—now such a great motive dower for manufacturing purposes ; when they considered the completion of the scheme for providing what he hoped would be a never-failing supply of tbe best and purest water both for drinking and sanitary purposf 8, t e thought they might fairly assume that the town and trade of Warrington were in a very fair con- dition• (Hear, hear.) There was one other featnre which he would like to refer to and that was that War- rington would bear a very fair comparison with other towns of its size from a sanitary standpoiot. The death rate was the means of ascertaining this particular point, and although Warrington might not be amongst the cleanest or the prettiest or sweetest of towns—though its atmosphere might be smoke polluted and sometimes impregnated by the emanations from the river and the Manchester Ship Canal whose waters did not compare with eau-de- Cologne—(laughter)—still notwithstanding these draw- backs Warrington might be considered a fairly healthy town as manufacturing towns went. THE WIDNES AND RUNCORN TRANSPOR TER. An Ofticial Contradiction of Rumours.

Readers will be aware that during the past fortnight there have been p(rsistent rumours that the foundations of the stone approaches on the Runcorn side of the Transporter Bridge had given way which would tracer- sarily involve the entire reconstruction of the structure. Risncorn had it that in consequence the work would be delayed at least six months and that the tons of con- crete already employed in the foundation work would have to be relayed. In consequence of these rumours, and in view of the financial interest which Runcorn and Widnes have in the Bridge scheme, our representative called upon the chief engineer to ascertain the truth of such reports. It was at once admitted by the official that there was a slight fissure in the stone struc- ture, apparent from the foreshore, caused by the settling of the foundations. There was, heweyer, absolutely no cause for alarm. The crack appeared some few weeks ago and had hitherto not become more pronounced than when it first appeared. The work, he was convinced, would not possibly be delayed lay the occurrence a single day. And there was no ground whatever for any alarmist reports. Ml that the wall required to put it to " rights " was a little additional pointing. It is PAtiP- factory to be assured on such positive grounds that the bridge is in no way in peril, and thus put an end to the Idle tales which had been so assiduously circulated.
