THE CORPORATION TRAMWAYS.

Report of the Town Clerk on the warrington Light Railway Order.

The following report has beer. prepared b 7 the Town Clerk on the Warrington Light Railway Order : "The Light Railway Commissioners have made ac Order authorising the construction of the light railways applied for by the Corporation in November last. The Order will shortly be submitted to the Board of Trade by whom it must be confirmed. The board before con- firming the Order will give public notice fixing the date by which objections to the order can be made. Objec- tions have already been made to the Light Railway Com- missioners against several < Iliuses in the Order. The main objections to the Order arise under the following lacads::— (1). Additional widening of roads, (2). The lighting of all posts wherever pl aced. (3). The fixing of id fare from Rylands-street to Victoria-sculare, Stockton Reath. (4). Keeping of separate accounts. (5). Financial provision.. , The estimate of the cost of constructing railway No. 1 I (put in at the inquiry) WRS £7,055. The street widen- ing.' include the purchased of the gardens and fore courts opposite St Thomas's Church and a strip of the field running on to the Mulberry Tree, also the six cottages adjoining the Mulberry Free and the fou houses opposite the Mulberry Tree. The estimated cos of acquiring this property is £2,125. With regard to road wieenings the following clause has been insert 5. (1). Concurrently with too constructi on of railway (No, 1) the Corporation shall widen the road upon which the same is to b 3 laid whenever it is shown by the limits of deviatiou on the Wang that land is to be acquired for that purpose to such extent that the metalled portion of the road shall not be less than thirts - five feed wide with a footpath not less than five feet wide on each side thereof. Provided that in places where the width available up to the said limits of devia- tion is insufficient for such widening, the Corporation ill make the metalled portion of the road n A less than I iiry-two feet wide, and shall also provide o, each side thereof a f lotpath of a width of six feet, or such a width up to six feet as may be got by acquiring lands or buildings or easements over them beyond such limits, but without incurring any unreasonable expense in 90 doing.' The first part of the Clause requires a road, including two footpaths of five feet each, of 45 feet in width. The proviso requires a roadway (with a footpath on each side of six feet) of 44 feet. Where the road there- fore can be widened it is to be made 45 feet in widet, and if the Corporation have to acquire property outside the limits of deviation then it is to be made 44 feet. The Clause provides that the Corporatioa shall not be put to unreasonable expense in such wideniogs but I must point out that the property outside the limits of deviation has not been scheduled and the Corporation have no compulsory powers of purchase and under the circumstances it is not probable that the Corporation cantacquire the property without incurring unreason- able expense. There are seven houses and shops which would have to be purchased to give the 44 feet and the Borough Surveyor has made an estimate of the cost of acquiring this property. It may be pointed out that the Light Railway Commissioners were prepared to grant the Warrington and Northwich Railway Company ats order to construct a single line of railway without any widen- ing of the road opposite Si Thomas's Church. The definition of 'road ' in the Order would include the swing bridges and unless the clause is amended it as ould throw upon the Corporation the obligation to widen the bridges. Clause 15.—Two years were allowed by the Order for the compulsory purchase of lands, but on application the commissioners have extended the period to three years. Clause 26.—1 f the road authority at any time decide to pave any road in front ot any public building, school or place of public worship with wood the Corporation are required by this clause to re-pave with wood the portion repairable by them in front of any such log, and for 50 feet beyond each end. At the inquiry the Corporation agreed with the read authority tore- pave with wood in front of any ' .rising,' buildings above referred to. Ido not understand why Use com- missioners should alter an agreed clauee. Clause 32 would impose upon the Corporation the obligation of providing and maintaining a " sufficient light "upon all posts erected upon any road or any foot- path from one hour after sunset tone hour before sun- rise. The Clause provides that tae lights shall not be required for the 'purpose of illuminaticn.' A small red light may therefore be sufficient. The Corporation agreed to light any post erected on the carriage way. The Runcorn Rural District Council now pay the Cor- poration fer the lighting of the roads. Clause 33 is inserted for the protection of the owners of bridges and as special clauses have been inserted for the protection of the Manchester Ship Canal Company with regard to the awing bridges the Commissioners have been asked to amend the clause sa that it will not apply to the swing budges. Clause 36 is inserted for the protection of the Man- chester Ship Canal Co., and imposes upon the Corpora- tion the obligation of improving the adjustment and alignment of the swing bridges, 'so far as may be essential for the safe working of the railway, and be- yond which is necessary for the existing road traffic.' In addition to this outlay there would be the annual expenses entailed to provide for signalling electric cur- rent therefor, the maintenance of the wood block pave- ment, scavenging, Stc. Clause 53 gives the Warrington and Northwich Light Railway Company the tiaht ' by agreement with the Corporation 'to use the • Railways' belonging to the Corporation. The terms of the agreement are to be subject to the approval of the Board of Trade. The Clause also gives that Company the right to use the ' Railway 'upon terms to be approved by the Board or determined by arbitration. The Order defines ' Rail- way ' to mean the light railways authorised by the order. The tiritno 'railways ' is not defined but it is clearly intended to include the tramways within the Borough. _ .

Clause 63 authorises a charge of id per mile, but it also provides that the Corporation shall not charge more than id • for each nassenger travelling only between Rylande-streec and Victoria-square.' This proviso is contrary to the decision of the OommissionArs expressed at the inquiry. In my opinion the Commissioners have no power to fix or alter the rates and charges allowed by the Tramways Order and therefore clause 63 so far as it affects the tramways from Rylands-street to Stafford-road is ultra viree.

Clause 67 fixes cheap fares for the labouring classes and allows fd per mile or fraction of that distance. No fare is fixed for the dotance between Rylandg-street and Victoria-square, -which is 11 furlongs, and the fare would therefore be id.

_ Clause 73 gives the County Council power to purchase the un&rtaking after the expiration of 30 years. The Corporation and the County Council agreed to a period of 35 years. Clauses 7d to 85.—The Commissioners have been asked to substitute the financial provisions of the Cor- poration Act of 1899 and the Local Government Board have reported in favour of that course. Clause 86 rrquires that senarate accounts shall be kept. This is almost impracticable and the Commis- sioners have been asked to make the Light Railways part of the Tramways undertaking. Under the agreement with the Warrington and North- wich Light Railway Co.,tbe Corporation undertook that if they required the Company to abandon railway No. 1 that they themselves should apply for powers to con- struct that railway and on failure of the Corporation to obtain such powers and to Commence the construc- tion, that the rower to construct such Railway should revert to the Company. The Light Railway Commis- sioners have issued an Order to that Ceimoany authoris- ing the construction of a railway from Victoria Square, Stockton Reath, to Northwich. _ .

. - It will be remeintered that the Corporation required the company to abandon Railway No. 1 because of the terms sought to be imposed by the Manchester Ship Canal Company with regard to the use of the swing bridges and in view of the onerous conditions imposed by the Warrington Ord.r it might be considered advis- able to allow the Warrington and Northwich Light Rail- way Company to resume the power to construct Rail- way No. 1 having regard to the fact that the terms with the Manchester Ship Canal Company with respect to the awing bridges have now been settled.

It may be safely estimated that when the Wilderspool tramway is opened for traffic 90 per cent. of the persons now using the 'buses between Stockton Heath and War- rington will use the cars mod willingly pay Li fare for the journey. If the Corporation continue the tramway to Vietoria—square it will mean an expenditure of nearly £ll,OOO without any material increase in the revenue. That amount spent in new tramways in the borough would provide for nearly two miles of single track com- plete. The money would be spent for the benefit of our own ratenayers, and would be a source of considerable revenue,"

THE HEADMASTER'S REPORT. HIGIIER EDUCATION.

The fireLDMAsTER, in presenting his annual report ettild : " My Lord Bishop, Ladies and Gentlemen,—The welcome we Rive you this afternoon is of special int?rest to myself, as it means that I have reached my majority as a Head Master. For 32 years I have been connected with this school, and for the last 21 years I have been privileged to be the director and organizer ot the Secon- dary school of this town. It follows as a matter of course that I have seen many changes in the past—not merely in coarection with the education in Warrington, but generally throughout the country. Perhaps nothing is more apparent than the vsst increase of interest in educational matters displayed by the general public. Years ago and perhaps even now it was the primary education of the country which aroused the deepest concern—but certainly in the last few years there has been • growing importance attached to sufficient and satisfactory provision of higher education, until at last the Government itself has realized the strength of the demand, and has brought in Bill dealing with higher education of every form. The future, therefore, is likely to see even greater changes than the past— and it is of the utmost importance that any change in the way of development or extension should at the out- set be directed on the lines of the highest efficiency. It would be a vast mistaece on the part of any authority not to meet the needs which exist in the community over which it has control—but efficiency whilst meeting the need fully will not make us blind to the value of money. What every community wants is the maximum of return for the minimum of outlay. To those who have watched, as [have done, the educational en- thusiasm in other towns, it is well known that schools have been built at great expense without there being first a full and accurate knowledge of the need to be satiefied. Many of our Technical schools, for example, in our towns under 100,000 population, although Of the greatest value for evening work, are hardly used at all curing the day time, nor is there material in these towns in the way of students to make such use of them. I do not think that the public are sufficiently well aware that in Germany and Switzerland—our foremost educa- tional rivals—there are very few Technical sehools indeed which admit students under the age of 16. I am saying this not in any way to damn enthusiasm in the pre- vision of better education—but a word of caution does seem necessary when the tales of German and American supremacy throw us almost into a panic. You will not take it amiss if I venture to offer a few remarks on the position of our own town, and I speak as an educa- tionist of considerable experience and as one who desires the truest welfare of the place where I have spent almost all the working years of my life.—Roughly ' speaking the Education Bill creates one authority for the whole of the education of a district with a view to first, the ample provision of schools and second, the cthol-otredvinersytineghildanshdalciohrraevleatiangsaotifsfsacchtooorlys. prTimhiasrmy eedanus. cation and that opportunity shall be afforded for thoie who need it of higher education. There are many parents who wish to give their children a year or two's further education than dab elementary schools provide—and to whom the education in a seceand- ary school would be impossible and useless ; for many of these children will be apprenticed to a trade. \ What seems desirable in their case is a school which will continue the work of the elementary echeol. It is a matter now of common consent that very very few hove indeed coning from an elementary school prove satie- factory students of the Day Technical Schools. On the other hand there are always a number of boys in the elementary school, who by their superior natural gilts can reap the full advantage of a Secondary School, if they entered sufficiently young,—and to whom no posi- tion in the ladder of education is unattainable. Many a boy has come to this Grammar School from the ele- mentary school and has by hie great ability and perseverance woe his way to the foremost rank. There are already connected with this school 8 scholarship 9 for the purpose of helping on deserving boys from the elementary schools,—but it is surely a fact beyond dispute that there are more boys than this in our primary schools who would be the better for the advantage of a Seconday Education. It is, I think, alao true that the Grammar School type of education might with regard to many boys be modified and broadeoed without in any way reducing its high standard. It is the business of a Grammar School to educate boys for the higher walks, the more responsible positinns of life, whether it be in commerce, the Civil Service, or the Professions. The number of such boys will always be comparatively small and the cost of educating them proportionately increas- ed, and this question of cost has prevented many schools from developing their commercial end sFientifie sides to the extent that head masters have desired. With the financial aid which may be a result under the Bill this difficulty will he to a large extent removed, and it seems possible, therefore, without depriving a boy's education of the character of a Grammar school education,to give It that special turn in the later years of school life as will be more directly in consonance with his future career. The Grammar Schools, it is acknowledged, provide the beat sort of material for the hither technical education, and Rive breadth and culture to the man who has to go out into the world and compete with his fellows in the way of business- These, then, are the three points which I think deserve serious consideration in dealing with the highi-r educa- tion question of this town. First, the provision of a higher elementary school for children who are destined to be apprenticed to trades—secondly, a scheme by which the gifted boys from our elementary schools may receive the higher education in a secondary school which their gifts demand—and thirdly, the financial assistance which will enable the secondary education of the town tee be put on a broader basis. The question of buildings, kc, comes second to and after the settlement of these main issues for whet ei-P have to consider first is not in what buildings shall we place our machinery, but what work do we wish our machinery lo turn out and what machinery we have alreadr. We want first a most careful inquiry as to the needs of the town— mean the number of boys who are likely to take advantage of a higher education as well as the character of the higher education required. next the knowledge how far these needs are being met or can to met by the existing schools. When once we have discovered the truth on these two paha!' it will not

be long, lam sure before satisfactory and ample pro- vision for doing the work is forthcoming. My only excuse fortaking up so much of your time is the gravity of the question and the necessity for approaching it from the mest economical as well as the most efficient way. Leaving now the general question of education on one side, it is with the greatest regret that I have to record the loss of one who has been connected with this school for the last 30 years—l refer to the late Mr Robert DavieS, Clerk to the Governing Body. He was a personal friend of my own, and I ever found in him ready sympathy and wise counsel in all matters pertaining to the school. Nor most I forget the late Mr. John Cros- field, for many years one of our Governors—who most generously supported the Scholarship Fund, which haa enabled many a boy to achieve his ambition and go to the University.—Before calling upon the Bishop to dis- tribute the prizes and certificat,,s. I shall now read you a summary of the Examiner's report on the school work of the past year, and the list of special honours gained during the year." The headmaster then read the examiner's report, which stated that as a result of a week's residence in the school, he could conscientiously report that the school was in a thoroughly satisfactory condition, both as regarded currioulum, organizati dis:ipline, and general tone. In congratulating the governors sad headmaster upon the efficient state of the school the examiner stated that with the inevitable reorganization of secondary education in the near future, it would be a great satisfaction for them, as well as the parents of the rising generation, to know that they al- ready had a school that would without doubt success- fully claim to be, as it had been in the past, the most important feature in the educational life of the district. (Applause). The Bina vOF LIVERPOOL distributed the prizes, and delivered an address. THE PRIZE LIST.

DEATH OF MR. JOHN EIVISON.

A Prominent Friendly Society Official.

Warrington has lost an esteemed townsman and la zealous Friendly Society official by the death of Mr John Emsou, which took place at his residence in Thynne-street on Monday evening. The deceased gentleman, who had been ailing for some time, was 58 years of age. lie was born on September 18th, ' 1843, at Great Budworth, near Sorthwich. When he was very young his parents removed to Knuts- ford,where he was educated at the Parochial School. When;about 13 years of age they came into the neighbourhood of Warrington, where he learned the business of a French polisher. In 1864, Mr Emson obtained a situation at Messrs Robert Gar- nett and Son's, cabinet makers, and after being there many years be accepted the position of master nolisher,which be held up to a short time ago,when he commenced business on his own account.

His first connection with a friendly society began 'at 19 years of age, when he was working in Man- chester, whcro he joined the Duchess of Devonshire Lodge of the National Independent Order of Odd- fellows. On leaving Manchester for Warrington he was transferred to the Garibaldi Lodge. In 1870 he also decided to become a member of the Admiral Nelson Lodge, connected with the Manchester Unity of Oddfellows and was duly initiated. He was soon put in office and mounting the ladder step by step be passed the chairs, after which he was appointed secretary of the lodge. He was also lodge secretary for some years of a Society in War- rington known as the Children's Burial Society, which was formed in March, 1868, for the benefit of Oddfellows. The lodge having sent him to re- present them at the district meetings, he was several times selected as district auditor and in 1882 be was elected deputy grand master of the dis- trict, and in 1883, grand master. On the death of the provincial U.S. (Mr John Houghton), in 1883, Mr Emson was elected to the position of C.S., an office which he held to the time of his death Mr Emson was also secretary for many years of the Garibaldi Lodge, N.1.0.F.,, and district secretary for 15 years of the Warrington district of the National Independent Order of Oddfellows. Mr Emson was favoured with a number of presents and testimonials for his services from the members of the Independent Order of Oddfellows,and from the Manchester Unity. The Garibaldi Lodge presented him with a very handsome ruby goblet, hasing the emblem of the Order engraved on it, and when he was appointed Provincial C.S, in the Manchester Unity, he resigned all offices in connection with the National Independent Order, and the members of that society in Warrington at once took measures to show their appreciation of his services. Their efforts culminated in his being invited to a public dinner and to his becoming the recipient of a beautiful tea and coffee service. Mrs Emson at the same time was presented with a cruet stand. In 1893, the jubilee of the Admiral Nelson Lodge was held, and it having leaked out that their secretary would also attain his jubilee at the same time, the members of both his lodge and district decided that he should not be forgotten. The result was that on his 50th birthday the lodge presented him with a massive silver ink-stank, and a past officer's certifi- cate, framed in carved oak. The members of the district at the same time presented him with a six guinea PP G Ma jewel, with a suitable inscription. In 1887, the Warrington district presented the late Provincial C S (Bro John Houghton) with a purse of gold, and Bro Emson, who acted as secretary to the committee, was presented by them with a beautiful silver pen and pencil case as a mark of their esteem and in appreciation of his services in connection with the presentation to his pre- decessor.

Bro. Emsots was ever a reformer. His first a on becoming secretary WA9 to see that his lodge woo regis- tered, and although he had many difficulties to face success crowned his efforts. The next step was to see that every member paid according to erale, which led to a lot of grumbling, but ae it was oee of the things, if not the thing, that was required to put the lodge in the way of becoming solvent, it was ultimately carried out. Another matter which was a bone of contention wit') Bro. Emsrm was the large amouct spent by some of the lodges in which was call.cd rent, hut this matter was very suceessfully dealt with by a committee, which, after working some time, obtained a commodious house in which • were placed the district offices. The house taken was once the residence of Dr Enfield, who was Tutor in Belles Lettres and Rector A'ademicv at the Warrington Academy in 1770. Bro. Ernson was honoured by his distriet in being sent to represent them at the following A.M.C.'s :—Notting- ham, Reading, Stafford, Gloucester, Ipswieh, Salisbury, Derby, Southampton, horthompton, and Swansea. At ' Northampton he was second highest at the poll for por- trait, an honour which he highly esteemed. Ben. Emsen was a member of the Gilbert Greenall Lodge, No. 1250, of Free and Accepted Masons. Mr. Eatsox was Chairman of the Warrington United Friendly Societies Cennoil and took a deep interest in ell matters affecting Friendly Societies. He was Chairman of the committee which had eiarge of the arrangements for the Coronation procession of Friendly Societies, and I in that capacity rendered ungrudging and very valuable services. The postponement of the procession, after so much careful preparation occasioned him keen regret. Mr Emsen appeared to have a great fondness for xectorial work, and he acted for several years as secre- tary for* children's treat, balls, Sce, in connection with the Warrington Volnnteers, of which he was a member fer 14 years, retiring with the rank of sergeant. The news of his death created profound sorrow among a very large circle of friends. He leaves a widow,three sons, and five daughters, with whom the deepest Sympathy is e pressed.

Lord Roberts' Last Letter.

Sir,—Many of your readers will have read Lord Roberts' excellent letter of a few weeks ago asking the public "to be careful in the nature of the welccms " to be given to our returning troops that it" may no take the form of treating the men to stimulants in public houses and in the streets, and thus lead them into excesses which must tend to degrade those whom the nation delights to honour." Wo are desirous of print- ing this letter in the forms of three sheet placards for the was, smaller ones tor shops and house windows, and handbills for general circulation. But, alas ! this is the holiday season, and as a result,our financial resources are very tow indeed ! May I remind your readers that when I made a sirn,lar appeal for help to print L-rd Roberts' first letter nearly two years ago they generously sent me g?.3, arid with assistance from our funds we printed and circulated 90,000 copies* As, thank Gcd, the war is at an end, and probably three- fourths of the troops Are returning, we should be thank- ful for £5O to do what we wish.

RUNCORN NATIONAL BOYS' SCHOOL.

Annual Distribution of Prizes.

List of Winners.

On Thursday at noon the first annual prize dia- tributon in Connection with the National Schools was held on the school premises in Church-street, There was a full muster of boys from the lowest to the highest standards with expectation " writ large" over each individual countenance. The Rev H B Firth, whose interest in boys is so well known, was in attendance with Mr. Littler, one of the school managers, and Mr A S Lindsay, A.C.P., head master, with his assietant teachers. Prior to the ceremony each of the boys was presented with a seealed letter from the neadmaeter containing a report of the pupil's progress in his educational work for ,the year ending June 30th ; a list of the successful prize winners for the year and an inter— esting letter on the year's work by Mr Arthur S Lindsay, the headmaster. The head master in (naming the proceedings remarked that that was the first time they had held a distribution in connection with the school. The prizes had been provided out of the fund created ty the scholars them- selves by reason of the concert held some time ago. On an occasion of that kind one regretted there were not prizes for all. Still were each to have a prize the honour attached to the winning of it would be some- what diminished. He was pleased to read them the report of his Majesty's Inspector which was as follows •—" Though the retirement of the late head master, Mr Jordan, who for so many years did such excellent service is much to be regretted, the school still maintains its high character under its new master." The examiner in religious knowledge says, " All show the resell; of excelit nt teaching in religious knowledge." They began their honours with gaining three town scholarships last July when Masters B Hilton, W Goff and F Allison proved themselves successful competitors. The headmaster, continuing, detailed the various successes gained by the school dwelling especially on the fact that the school had carried off the greatest distinction of the year in winning the only four new- Science and Art Scholarships offered for the first time in the district. The value of each was £36 distributed thus—£9, for the first year ; £l2, second year ; £l5, third year. These four scholars were indebted to Mr Littler, an old scholar and now a manager of the school, for the prizs they would receive in acknowledgement of the honour they had won. The success of the school was due to a very great extent to tne very hearty co- operation of the teachers. There were three special prizes that morning for the three boys who bad written the best composition on their educational excursion to Liverpool. The winners were William Warburton,George Ackerly, and Fred Yowd. (Applause.) 011 Ir Littler, who wrs to present the prizes, needed no introduction. Wherever boys were there was hibp the midst of them. (Applause.) dlr Lhtier, who was received very warmly, in the course of an instructive speech told the scholars that he was an old boy from the National Schools. It was at least thirty years ago since he was a scholar there and at that time there was only another boy and himself above standard five, which was in marked contrast to the 130 boys now occupying the two higher standards. Some people were always making comparisons betwe,n their own countries and foreign countries to the detri- ment of the former. But there was no doubt that if the British lad only made up his mind to do his work no one could possibly beat him. He appealed to them to be sincere and unselfish in their work, and to cherish a love for their sohool which should never be forgotten. The boys then received their prizes from the hands of Mr John Littler, each boy receiving an ovation as he stepped forward to receive his gift. The following were the successful students :

SENSATIONAL ENGLISH COLLAPSE.

Century by Trumper.

The fourth test match was commenced at Trafford on Thursday, when 18,000 people gathered to witness the play. Darling was fortunate in winning the toss on a soft wicket, and at lunch time the score of the visitors was 173 for one wicket (Duff's). Later, when the sun came nut, the innings was quickly finished off, the total reaching 299, of which Trumpet was responsible for a brilliant 104. When England went in there was a sensational collapse, 5 wickets falling for 44 runs against the bowling of Trumpet and Hopkins. Subse- quently Jackson and Braund played out time and the tbtal crept up to 70 runs. The full scbre is as fellows:—

At the St. Helens Police Court, on Monday, Richard Percy Walinesiey, the well-htown racing cyclist, of 29, Church-street, Preeton, was summoned on a charge of travelling between Manchester and St. Helens Junction without a ticket with intent to defraud. There was a further charge of giving a false name and addreQs, IC was as, stated that on the 30:th Mt., Coe ckiiendant was finsd £1 and £3 14s 61 costs for havi,g, on krisrols 1 last, travelled from Huston Station to Stockpor'; with- out having previously paid his fare. Mr Fenna, who prosecuted on behalf of the London avid North-Western Railway Company said the defen- dant en the 19th of June attended the St Helens Cycle Club sports. and his name was given on the programme as a competitor. He was specially watched, and was first seen on the 19th of June at Exchange Station. Mancheeter.lße arrived there at 5 10 p.no.,put his bicycle in the cupboard of the 5.15 train and then got into the train. He did not go to the booking office for a ticket as be had plenty of time t 3 do. Detective Sergeant Kynastim rode in the train and Saw defendant alight at St Helens Junction and get into the St Helens train. with which Kynaston also travelled. On arrival at St Helens defendant took out his bicncle and was passing through the barrier when the collector asked him for his ticket. He said, " Wait t moment, until I put Imy bicycle on one ale and I will give you the ticket." Ho made a show of producing the ticket, but said he could not find it, and banded the collector Scl, which he-said was the fare from St Helena Junction. Kytaston, who was dealing with another man, beard defendant say, " I have well come from Manchester for threepence." Kyaaston .followed defendant, and asked him for his nacre and address. Ha gave the name of J R Quayle, 74, Lady Barn-lane. Fallowfielci, which, Mr Fenna said, was a meaner thing than the fraud, as it was the name of a fellow-com- petitor. Evidence having been given, the defendant in plead- ing guilty to Use offences said it was a silly thing to do. The defendant was ordered to pay a fine of £lO and £1 13s 6d costs for travelling without paying his fare, or to go to gaol for twenty-eight days, and for giving a false address he was fined £2 acd 12s 61 costs, with the alternative penalty of twenty-eight days' imprison. mett. QUARTERLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON FOR QUARTER ENDING 30 CH JUNE, 1902. Durirg: the quarter the following births and deaths, were reerstcreo : Warrington registration district : births, 598 ; deaths 272. The excess of births over deaths was 326. The birth rate was equal to 363 per 1,000. The death-rate from allcauses was equal to 16 5 per 1,000 against 163 that of the previous quarter. The death- rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to .6 per 1,000, against '9 the rate of the previous quarter. The ages of mortality were:- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 op. Total. 79 31 12 12 98 40 272 The deaths included :—Scarlet lever, 2 ; irfluenza, 6; whooping cough, 5 ; diphtheria, 1 ; croup, 1 ; enteric fever, 3 ; diarrhcea, 1 ; phthisis, 19 ; other forms of tuberculosis, 10 ; cancer, 9 ; alcoholism, 2 ; old age, 26; cirrhosis of liver, 4 ; accidents. 9 ; all other causes, 174. The following eases of infectious disease have been reported daring this week :—h'carler fever,47;dipbtheria. 8 ; enterio fover,*B ; erysipelas. 30 ; phthisis, 15. J. GUEST GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health. CHARGE AGAINST MR. E. L. FLEMING IN NEW YORK: Invoicing Borax as Soda. A " Central INews " telegram states that Mr Ernest L Fleming, of Weaverharn, Cheshire, expor- ter of washing crystal., was charged at New York on Thursday last week with falsely invoicing borax as soda. He was remanded on bail in .Csoo.The duty on imported borax is ten times as much as the duty on soda. Most of our readers will remember Mr Fleming as the manager for Messrs Bishop and Company, Limited, and secretary to the Municipal Non-Trading Committee.

THE WATER DIFFICULTY. An Ample Supply. The temporary pump which was got to work last week end at the new wells in Delph-lane has been pumping continuously during the past week and the result is that the height of the water in the reservoir at Winwick has risen from 5 feet to 16 feet. A second temporary pump arrived yesterday from Messrs Simpson and Company's works and it is expected that it will be at work early next week. The Corporation will then have a temporary plant at Delph-lane capable of pumping one million gallons per day. This additional supply will be ample to meet all requirements. The permanent plant will be ready for use before the close of the year
